## A Block-encoding and state-preparation constructions

Here we provide circuits for block-encoding and state preparation of various matrices and vectors used in our analysis. We assume access to the  $(\alpha = 1, a)$ -block-encoding  $U_A$  of A and the state preparation unitary  $U_b$ , as well as their inverses and controlled versions. Our constructions also involve other gates such as single-qubit rotation gates and multi-controlled Toffoli gates. For a projector  $\Pi$  we denote  $C_{\Pi}NOT$  as the operation that flips an ancilla bit (by applying a Pauli-X gate) conditioned on being in the image of  $\Pi$ , and otherwise applying the identity.

$$\CPiNOT = \Pi \otimes X + (I-\Pi) \otimes I_2$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 742 -->

In quantum circuits, we draw this with a  $\Pi$  box on the first register controlling a target  $\oplus$  on the ancilla. When  $\Pi = |e_j\rangle\langle e_j|$ , this is a multi-controlled Toffoli gate, with the control bits set to  $|0\rangle\langle 0|$  or  $|1\rangle\langle 1|$  depending on the binary representation of j.

We denote single qubit rotations by  $e^{i\theta P} = \cos(\theta)I + i\sin(\theta)P$  where  $P \in \{X, Y, Z\}$  is a Pauli matrix. Note that  $e^{i\theta Y}|0\rangle = \cos(\theta)|0\rangle + \sin(\theta)|1\rangle$ .

Furthermore, we recall the identification of the identity matrix of dimension d with s-qubit operators, via  $I_d = \sum_{j=0}^{d-1} |e_j\rangle\langle e_j|$ .

#### A.1 Block-encoding of G

The (1, a+1)-block-encoding of  $G = Q_b A$  is depicted in Figure 3. It has normalization 1 and involves a+1 ancilla qubits. It costs one query to  $U_A$ ,  $U_b$  and  $U_b^{\dagger}$ , as well as one multi-controlled Toffoli gate.

Figure 3: Quantum circuit implementing (1, a + 1)-block-encoding of G.

To verify the correctness of the circuit, note that the final three gates implement  $I_2 \otimes I_{2^a} \otimes (I_{2^s} - |\boldsymbol{b}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{b}|) + X \otimes I_{2^a} \otimes |\boldsymbol{b}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{b}|$ . By sandwiching this operation with  $\langle 0|\cdot |0\rangle$  on the first qubit, the second term vanishes, and we see it constitutes a block-encoding of  $I_{2^s} - \boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{\dagger}$  with block-encoding factor 1. The whole circuit then is a simple product of block-encodings for A and for  $I_{2^s} - \boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{\dagger}$ . We then conclude by noting that since  $\boldsymbol{e_j}^{\dagger}A = 0$  for all  $j \geq m$ , we have  $G = Q_{\boldsymbol{b}}A = (I_m - \boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{\dagger})A = (I_{2^s} - \boldsymbol{b}\boldsymbol{b}^{\dagger})A$ .

#### A.2 Block-encoding of $A_t$

The (1, a+1)-block-encoding of  $A_t = A + t^{-1} |e_m\rangle\langle e_n|$  is shown in Figure 4. It uses one controlled query to  $U_A$ , two multi-controlled Toffoli gates, one controlled-rotation gate, and up to s CNOT gates. It requires precomputing the angle  $\arccos(1/t)$ , based on the choice of t. We can verify the circuit by setting the input and output of the ancillas

Figure 4: Quantum circuit implementing (1, a+1)-block-encoding of  $A_t$ . The controlled- $\bigoplus_{m-n}$  gate denotes a series of at most s CNOT gates controlled by the first register and acting on different bits of the final s-qubit register, which transform  $|e_n\rangle$  into  $|e_m\rangle$  if the control bit is set to 1.

to  $|0\rangle$  and considering possible input and output states on the s-qubit system. If the input state is not  $|e_n\rangle$ , then  $U_A$ 

## PDF page 17


is applied and A is enacted on the s-qubit system. Since  $\langle e_{\boldsymbol{m}}|A|e_{\boldsymbol{j}}\rangle=0$  for all indices j, the final gate does not flip the first ancilla. Meanwhile, if the input state is  $|e_{\boldsymbol{n}}\rangle$  and the output state is  $|e_{\boldsymbol{m}}\rangle$ , then the first ancilla is turned to  $|1\rangle$  by the first gate and returned to  $|0\rangle$  by the last gate. Prior to being returned to  $|0\rangle$ , it controls a single-qubit rotation gate that sends  $|0\rangle \mapsto t^{-1}|0\rangle + \sqrt{1-t^{-2}}|1\rangle$ , and it also controls a set of NOT gates that transform the input  $|e_{\boldsymbol{n}}\rangle$  into the output  $|e_{\boldsymbol{m}}\rangle$ . Postselecting this ancilla on  $|0\rangle$  verifies the correct matrix entry  $t^{-1}$  for  $|e_{\boldsymbol{m}}\rangle\langle e_{\boldsymbol{n}}|$ . Finally, if the input is  $|e_{\boldsymbol{n}}\rangle$  and the output is not  $|e_{\boldsymbol{m}}\rangle$ , then the matrix element is seen to be zero, as expected.

#### A.3 State preparation unitary $U_{b'}$

The state preparation unitary that prepares  $|\mathbf{b'}\rangle = (|\mathbf{b}\rangle + |\mathbf{e_m}\rangle)/\sqrt{2}$  uses one ancilla qubit that begins and ends in  $|0\rangle$ . It also requires one controlled query to  $U_b$  and a controlled- $U_{e_m}$  gate that prepares  $|\mathbf{e_m}\rangle$  controlled on an ancilla—this can be accomplished with up to s CNOT gates (depending on the binary representation of m). Finally, it utilizes one multi-controlled Toffoli gate to disentangle the ancilla after the controlled operations—to verify this action, recall that  $\langle \mathbf{b}|\mathbf{e_m}\rangle = 0$ .

#### A.4 Block-encoding of $G_t$

The (1, a+2)-block-encoding of  $G_t = Q_{b'}A_t$  is depicted in Figure 6. It is the same as the block-encoding of G from Figure 3, except with  $U_A$  replaced by  $U_{A_t}$  and  $U_{b'}$  replaced by  $U_{b'}$ .

Figure 6: Quantum circuit implementing (1, a + 2)-block-encoding of  $G_t$ .

## A.5 Block-encoding of $\bar{A}_{\sigma}$

The (1, a+1)-block-encoding of the matrix  $\bar{A}_{\sigma}$  is the most complex, and depicted in Figure 7. It uses one controlled query to  $U_A$ , one CNOT gate, one single-qubit rotation gate, and one  $C_{\Pi}NOT$  gate with  $\Pi = I_{2^s} - I_m$ . The  $C_{\Pi}NOT$  gate could be further decomposed into poly(s) Toffoli and CNOT gates, for example, by comparing the binary representation of the input state  $|e_j\rangle$  to that of m, starting with the most significant digit, and computing whether  $m \geq j$  into the third register depicted in the figure. This strategy would require at least 1 additional (unshown) ancilla qubit which would then need to be uncomputed.

To enact the single-qubit rotation, we precompute the angle 
$$\varphi = \arccos\left(\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^2}\right)$$
 and note that  $e^{-i\varphi Y}|0\rangle = \sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^2}|0\rangle - f(\sigma)|1\rangle$ , and  $e^{-i\varphi Y}|1\rangle = f(\sigma)|0\rangle + \sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^2}|1\rangle$ .

To verify the correctness of the circuit, recall that here we view the matrix  $\bar{A}_{\sigma}$  as an (s+1)-qubit operator equivalent to  $\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^2}|0\rangle\langle 0|\otimes A+f(\sigma)|0\rangle\langle 1|\otimes I_m$ , which acts on the final two registers of the circuit. The first two gates are seen to enact a block-encoding of the operator  $|0\rangle\langle 0|\otimes A+|1\rangle\langle 1|\otimes I_m$ , using the second and third registers as block-encoding ancillas. Meanwhile, the final two gates are seen to enact a block-encoding of the operator  $|0\rangle(\sqrt{1-f(\sigma)^2}\langle 0|+f(\sigma)\langle 1|)\otimes I_{2^s}$ , using the first register as a block-encoding ancilla—this is because sandwiching the first qubit between  $\langle 0|\cdot |0\rangle$  postselects on both the input and output of the CNOT gate being  $|0\rangle$ .

The correctness of the circuit is then verified by multiplying the operators block-encoded by these two parts of the circuit.

## PDF page 18


Figure 7: Quantum circuit implementing (1, a + 1)-block-encoding of  $\bar{A}_{\sigma}$ , which acts on a s + 1-qubit system.

#### A.6 How to pad A in the first place

In Section 2, we assumed we are given an instance (A, b) of the QLSP of size  $m \times n$ , and a block-encoding  $U_A$  that views A as an operator on s qubits with  $\max(m,n) < 2^s$ . This means there is at least one row and column of zero padding built in. Here we drop this assumption, and assume that  $\max(m,n) = 2^s$ . This means that in order to augment the linear system we need to introduce a new basis vector  $|e_m\rangle = |e_n\rangle$ , and this requires introducing a whole new qubit and expanding the total vector space to size  $2^{s+1}$ . To fit this situation into our framework, we now give an alternative block-encoding of A that simply pads it with zeros as  $\begin{bmatrix} A & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$  when viewed as an (s+1)-qubit operator rather than as an s-qubit operator, so that  $\max(m,n) < 2^{s+1}$ . This construction uses a+1 total ancillas, a multi-controlled Toffoli and a CNOT gate. It uses one call to  $U_A$  but no calls to controlled  $U_A$ . This construction could then be used in place of  $U_A$  everwhere else that  $U_A$  appears, causing the substitution  $a \mapsto a+1$ ,  $s \mapsto s+1$ . To see that this construction is correct, note the following sequence of logic. First, sandwiching the a-qubit register

Figure 8: Quantum circuit implementing (1, a) = (1, a + 1)-block-encoding of A as an operator on s + 1 qubits (padded with zeros), given as input a (1, a)-block-encoding of A as an operator on s qubits.

with  $\langle 0|\cdot|0\rangle$  triggers the multi-controlled Toffoli that flips the first qubit. Then, sandwhiching the first qubit with  $\langle 0|\cdot|0\rangle$  requires a second flip to occur, meaning nonzero output is only possible if the final qubit is in  $|0\rangle$ .

## B Implementation of kernel projection and kernel reflection

#### B.1 Quantum singular value tranformation

The formalism of quantum singular value transformation (QSVT) [20] allows for polynomial transformations of the singular values of a block-encoded matrix.

Let  $U_B$  be an  $(1, a_B)$ -block-encoding of a matrix  $B = \sum_{j=0}^{2^s-1} \sum_{k=0}^{2^s-1} b_{jk} |e_j\rangle\langle e_k|$  (viewed as an operator on s qubits with appropriate zero-padding) and let  $B = W\Sigma V^{\dagger}$  be a singular value decomposition (SVD) of B, where  $\Sigma$  is the diagonal matrix of singular values. Let P be a real, even, degree-d polynomial for which  $|P(x)| \leq 1$  whenever  $|x| \leq 1$  (QSVT also works for odd polynomials, but we only need even polynomials in our application). Corollary 18 of Ref. [20, arXiv version] states that we can find a quantum circuit implementing  $U_P^{(B)}$ , which is a  $(1, a_B + 1)$ -block-encoding of  $VP(\Sigma)V^{\dagger}$ :

$$V P(\Sigma) V^\dagger = (\bra{0}^{\otimes(a_B+1)} \otimes I_{2^s}) U_{P}^{(B)}(\ket{0}^{\otimes(a_B+1)} \otimes I_{2^s})\,,$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 910 -->

The unitary  $U_P^{(B)}$  uses d/2 calls to each of  $U_B$  and  $U_B^{\dagger}$ , 2d multi-controlled Toffoli gates, and d single-qubit rotation gates. The circuit for  $U_P^{(B)}$  is given in Figure 9.

## PDF page 19


Figure 9: Quantum circuit implementing the QSVT unitary  $U_P^{(B)}$ . The phases  $\phi_j$  must be chosen to implement the polynomial P. Conditioned on the input and output of the first  $a_B + 1$  qubits being  $|0\rangle^{\otimes (a_B + 1)}$ , the operation  $VP(\Sigma)V^{\dagger}$  is applied to the state on the third register, where  $B = W\Sigma V^{\dagger}$  is a SVD of B. The gate H denotes Hadamard.

#### B.2 Kernel projection

We can apply QSVT to perform eigenstate filtering (EF) [8], or more precisely in our application, kernel projection (KP). This technique gives a procedure to project onto the kernel of a matrix by constructing an appropriate polynomial filter function and applying QSVT. Specifically, given target parameters  $\Delta \in (0,1)$  and  $\eta \in (0,1]$ , we take


$$\ell = \left\lceil \frac{\arccosh(\eta^{-1})}{\arccosh\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right)} \right\rceil \leq \left\lceil \frac{1}{2\Delta}\ln\left(\frac{2}{\eta}\right) \right\rceil$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 941 -->

and we define the even, degree- $2\ell$  polynomial  $F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)$  given in Ref. [8]

$$F_{\Delta, \ell}(x) = \frac{T_\ell\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2-2x^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right)}{T_\ell\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right)} \tag{52}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 945 -->

where  $T_{\ell}$  is the  $\ell$ th Cheybshev polynomial of the first kind, given by

$$T_\ell(z) = \begin{cases}
        \cos(\ell \arccos(z)) & \text{if } |z|\leq 1 \\
        \cosh(\ell \arccosh(z)) & \text{if } z >1 \\
        (-1)^\ell \cosh(\ell \arccosh(z)) & \text{if } z < -1
    \end{cases}\,. \tag{53}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 949 -->

This polynomial is plotted in Figure 10.

Figure 10: Example of  $F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)$  (kernel projection) and  $R_{\Delta,\ell}(x)$  (kernel reflection) for  $\Delta = 0.1$  and  $\ell = 21$ , implying  $\eta \leq 0.03$ .

**Lemma 1.** The polynomial  $F_{\Delta,\ell}$  is guaranteed to satisfy

## PDF page 20


- 1. For all  $x \in [-1,1]$ , it holds that  $|F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)| \leq 1$
- 2. For all  $x \in [\Delta, 1]$ , it holds that  $|F_{\Delta, \ell}(x)| \leq T_{\ell}(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2})^{-1} \leq \eta$ .
- 3.  $F_{\Delta \ell}(0) = 1$ .

*Proof.* This builds on and improves upon Lemma 13 of Ref. [8].<sup>7</sup> Note that the Chebyshev polynomial  $T_{\ell}(z)$  is bounded on [-1,1] when  $z \in [-1,1]$ . Moreover, for  $z \geq 1$  it is monotonically increasing. Thus, the numerator achieves its maximum on  $x \in [-1,1]$  when x = 0, where  $F_{\Delta,\ell} = 1$  by construction, verifying properties 1 and 3. To verify Property 2, note that for all  $x \in [\Delta,1]$ , the numerator is bounded in the range [-1,1]. Moreover, the absolute value of the denominator evaluates to

$$\begin{aligned}T_\ell\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right) = \cosh\left(\ell \arccosh\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right)\right)\end{aligned} \tag{54}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 972 -->

We immediately see that if  $\ell \geq \operatorname{arccosh}(\eta^{-1})/\operatorname{arccosh}((1+\Delta^2)/(1-\Delta^2))$ , then the denominator is larger than  $\eta^{-1}$ .

In other words,  $F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)$  is a filter of width  $\Delta$ , which maps the zero input to one and all inputs outside the window  $[-\Delta, \Delta]$  to a value close to zero. In fact, the polynomial  $F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)$  is the optimal filter polynomial of degree  $2\ell$  in the sense of minimizing the maximum of  $|F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)|$  outside the window  $[-\Delta, \Delta]$  subject to  $F_{\Delta,\ell}(0) = 1$  [8, Appendix E].

We can apply QSVT using the polynomial  $F_{\Delta,\ell}$ . Suppose we have a  $(1,a_B)$ -block-encoding of  $B = W\Sigma V^{\dagger}$ , which has no singular values in the interval  $(0,\Delta)$ . We apply KP to a state  $|\phi\rangle = c_1|a\rangle + c_2|b\rangle$ , where a is in the kernel of B and b is orthogonal to the kernel. The vector  $|b\rangle$  has a decomposition  $\sum_j w_j |u_j\rangle$  into right singular vectors  $|u_j\rangle$  of B all of which have singular values  $\varsigma_j$  in  $(\Delta, 1)$ . By Property 2 of Lemma 1, for every j,  $F_{\Delta,\ell}(\varsigma_j)$  lies in the interval  $[-\eta, \eta]$ . Thus, the application of  $VF_{\Delta,\ell}(\Sigma)V^{\dagger}$  maps


$$\begin{aligned}\ket{\vec{b}}\mapsto \sum_j w_j F_{\Delta,\ell}(\varsigma_j) \ket{\vec{u_j}} = \delta_1\ket{\vec{b}} + \delta_2\ket{\vec{b'}}\end{aligned} \tag{55}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 982 -->

where b' is orthogonal to b and to the kernel of B, and  $\delta_1^2 + \delta_2^2 \leq \eta^2$ . This allows us to assert


$$U_{F_{\Delta,\ell}}^{(B)}(\ket{0}^{\otimes(a_B+1)} \otimes \ket{\phi}) = \underbrace{\left[\ket{0}^{\otimes(a_B+1)} \otimes (c_1  \ket{\vec{a}} + c_2\delta_1\ket{\vec{b}} + c_2\delta_2 \ket{\vec{b'}})\right]}_{\text{success}}+ \underbrace{|c_2|\sqrt{1-\delta_1^2-\delta_2}\ket{{\perp}}}_{\text{failure}} \tag{56}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 986 -->

where  $|\perp\rangle$  is a normalized state for which  $(\langle 0|^{\otimes(a_B+1)}\otimes I_{2^s})|\perp\rangle=0$ . Postselecting on the first register being  $|0\rangle^{\otimes(a_B+1)}$ , this implements an approximate projector onto the kernel of B. Accordingly, we can detect the success of KP by measuring the  $a_B+1$  ancillas and checking if they are all  $|0\rangle$ .

In our application, the matrix B is given by  $Q_{\mathbf{b}}A$  or  $Q_{\mathbf{b}'}A_t$ . The number of ancillas in these two cases is  $a_B = a + 1$  and  $a_B = a + 2$ , respectively, where a is the number of ancillas for the block-encoding  $U_A$ . In both cases, we can give a  $(1, a_B)$ -block-encoding of B using one (potentially controlled) query to each of  $U_A$ ,  $U_{\mathbf{b}}$ , and  $U_{\mathbf{b}}^{\dagger}$ , as illustrated in Appendix A.

Lemma 2 (Using KP to refine QLSS solution). Suppose  $\boldsymbol{b}$  is in the column space of A, and let  $\boldsymbol{x}$  denote the solution of minimum  $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|$  to the equation  $A\boldsymbol{x} = \boldsymbol{b}$ . Suppose A has no singular values in the interval  $(0, \kappa^{-1})$  and let  $\tilde{\rho}$  be a mixed quantum state for which  $\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\tilde{\rho}|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle = 1 - \mu^2$  and  $\langle \boldsymbol{y}|\tilde{\rho}|\boldsymbol{y}\rangle = 0$  for all  $\boldsymbol{y}$  that are in the kernel of A. Suppose KP is applied to approximately project  $\tilde{\rho}$  onto the kernel of  $G = Q_{\boldsymbol{b}}A$  using parameters  $(\kappa, \eta)$ . That is, we choose  $\ell$  using Eq. (51) with  $\Delta = \kappa^{-1}$  and apply the unitary  $U_{F_{\kappa^{-1},\ell}}^{(G)}$ , and then measure the ancillas to determine success. KP succeeds with probability at least  $1 - \mu^2$ , and when it succeeds it outputs a state  $\tilde{\rho}_{\text{out}}$  for which

$$\begin{aligned}\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}_{\rm out}\ket{\vec{x}} &\geq 1-\frac{\mu^2\eta^2}{1-\mu^2+\mu^2\eta^2} \geq 1-\frac{\mu^2\eta^2}{1-\mu^2}\end{aligned} \tag{57}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 997 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        %
    \frac{1}{2}\nrm{\ketbra{\vec{x}}-\tilde{\rho}_{\rm out}}_1 &\leq \frac{\mu \eta}{\sqrt{1-\mu^2+\mu^2\eta^2}} \leq \frac{\mu\eta}{\sqrt{1-\mu^2}}\end{aligned} \tag{58}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 997 -->



$$(58)$$

<sup>&</sup>lt;sup>7</sup>The lemma stated here has a constant prefactor for  $\ell$  that is better than that of Lemma 13 of Ref. [8] by a factor of  $\sqrt{2}$ . Additionally, we no longer require the restriction  $\Delta \in (0, 1/\sqrt{12}]$ . We acknowledge communication with Yu Tong on how the constant prefactor in their bound could be improved, a fact that was also pointed out in Ref. [10, Section V].

## PDF page 21


Proof. Let  $\tilde{\rho} = \sum_i p_i |\phi_i\rangle\langle\phi_i|$  be a decomposition of  $\tilde{\rho}$  as an ensemble of pure states. We have  $\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\tilde{\rho}|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle = \sum_i p_i |\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\phi_i\rangle|^2 = 1 - \mu^2$ . Referencing Eq. (56), here we have  $|\boldsymbol{a}\rangle = |\boldsymbol{x}\rangle$  and  $c_1 = |\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\phi_i\rangle|$ . Let  $|\psi_i\rangle$  denote the output state when KP acts on  $|\phi_i\rangle$  and we postselect on success, and let  $q_i$  be the probability of success. We can see that  $q_i \geq |c_1|^2 = |\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\phi_i\rangle|^2$ . We can also see that  $|\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\psi_i\rangle|^2 = |c_1|^2/q_i$ . The overall probability of success of KP is given by  $\sum_i p_i q_i \geq \sum_i p_i |\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\phi_i\rangle|^2 = 1 - \mu^2$ , showing the first claim of the lemma. Again referring to Eq. (56), we have  $|\delta_1|^2 + |\delta_2|^2 \leq \eta^2$ , so we can also say  $q_i \leq |\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\phi_i\rangle|^2 + \eta^2(1 - |\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\phi_i\rangle|^2)$ . The output state  $\tilde{\rho}_{\text{out}}$  is given by

$$\tilde{\rho}_{\rm out} = \frac{\sum_i p_i q_i \ketbra{\psi_i}}{\sum_i p_i q_i} \tag{59}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1006 -->

where including the denominator ensures the state is normalized. We now compute

$$\begin{aligned}\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}_{\rm out}\ket{\vec{x}} &= \frac{\sum_i p_i q_i |\braket{\vec{x}}{\psi_i}|^2}{\sum_i p_i q_i} = \frac{\sum_i p_i  |\braket{\vec{x}}{\phi_i}|^2}{\sum_i p_i q_i} =\frac{1-\mu^2}{\sum_i p_i q_i} \end{aligned} \tag{60}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1010 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        &\geq \frac{1-\mu^2}{\sum_i p_i (|\braket{\vec{x}}{\phi_i}|^2 +\eta^2(1-|\braket{\vec{x}}{\phi_i}|^2))}= \frac{1-\mu^2}{\eta^2 + (1-\mu^2)(1-\eta^2)} = 1-\frac{\mu^2\eta^2}{1-\mu^2+\mu^2\eta^2}\end{aligned} \tag{61}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1010 -->

The trace distance bound then follows from the general relationship  $\frac{1}{2}||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{x}|-\sigma|| \leq \sqrt{1-\langle\boldsymbol{x}|\sigma|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle}$  for any state  $\sigma$  [39, Eq. (9.110)].

#### B.3 Kernel reflection

We can extend the technique of kernel projection to kernel reflection. In kernel reflection, rather than preserving the kernel and (approximately) zeroing out its orthogonal complement, we preserve the kernel and (approximately) apply a -1 phase to the orthogonal complement. We do so within the framework of QSVT by constructing a new polynomial  $K_{\Delta,\ell}$ . Again, we are given parameters  $\Delta$  and  $\eta$ , and we choose  $\ell$  as in Eq. (51). We define the  $2\ell$ -degree polynomial by scaling and shifting  $F_{\Delta,\ell}$  to cover the range [-1,1]:

$$K_{\Delta,\ell}(x) = \frac{2F_{\Delta,\ell}(x)-1+F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}{1+F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)} = \frac{2T_{\ell}(\frac{1+\Delta^2-2x^2}{1-\Delta^2})+2}{T_{\ell}(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2})+1}-1\,. \tag{62}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1020 -->

 $K_{\Delta,\ell}$  is a degree- $2\ell$ , even polynomial.

**Lemma 3.** The polynomial  $K_{\Delta,\ell}(0)$  is guaranteed to satisfy

- 1. For all  $x \in [-1,1]$ , it holds that  $|K_{\Delta,\ell}(x)| \leq 1$
- 2. For all  $x \in [\Delta, 1]$ , it holds that  $|K_{\Delta, \ell}(x)| \le -1 + \frac{4}{T_{\ell}(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{\Delta^2})+1} \le -1 + \frac{4\eta}{1+\eta}$ .
- 3.  $K_{\Delta,\ell}(0) = 1$ .

*Proof.* Property 3 is readily verified by plugging in x=0. Property 1 follows from the fact that for  $x \in [-1,1]$ , the maximum of  $T_{\ell}(\frac{1+\Delta^2-2x^2}{1-\Delta^2})$  is achieved at x=0 and the minimum is -1 (as noted in proof of Lemma 1). To verify Property 2, first note that for all  $x \in [\Delta, 1]$ ,  $T_{\ell}(\frac{1+\Delta^2-2x^2}{1-\Delta^2}) \in [-1, 1]$ . Moreover, the denominator evaluates to

$$\begin{aligned}T_\ell\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right) +1  = \cosh\left(\ell \arccosh\left(\frac{1+\Delta^2}{1-\Delta^2}\right)\right) + 1\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1035 -->

We immediately see that if  $\ell \geq \operatorname{arccosh}(\eta^{-1})/\operatorname{arccosh}((1+\Delta^2)/(1-\Delta^2))$ , then the denominator is larger than  $\eta^{-1} + 1 = \eta/(1+\eta)$ , which completes the verification.

We can apply QSVT using the polynomial  $K_{\Delta,\ell}$ . Suppose we have a  $(1, a_B)$ -block-encoding of B, which has no singular values in the interval  $(0, \Delta)$ . We apply kernel reflection to a state  $|\phi\rangle = c_1 |a\rangle + c_2 |b\rangle$ , where a is in the

## PDF page 22


kernel of B and b is orthogonal to the kernel. Building on Eq. (55), we have

$$\begin{aligned}\ket{\vec{b}}\mapsto \sum_j w_j K_{\Delta,\ell}(\varsigma_j) \ket{\vec{u_j}} &= \sum_j w_j \frac{2F_{\Delta,\ell}(\varsigma_j)-1+F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}{1+F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)} \ket{\vec{u_j}}  \end{aligned} \tag{63}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1044 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
    &= - \left(1-\frac{2F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}{1+F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}\right)\ket{\vec{b}}+ \frac{2}{1+F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}(\delta_1\ket{\vec{b}} + \delta_2\ket{\vec{b'}}) \end{aligned} \tag{64}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1044 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
    &= -(1-\delta_1') \ket{\vec{b}} + \delta_2' \ket{\vec{b'}}\end{aligned} \tag{65}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1044 -->


$$= -(1 - \delta_1')|\boldsymbol{b}\rangle + \delta_2'|\boldsymbol{b'}\rangle \tag{66}$$

where  $\delta_1' = \frac{2F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta) + 2\delta_1}{1 + F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}$  and  $\delta_2' = \frac{2\delta_2}{1 + F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)}$ . We know that  $\eta \geq F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)$ ; if we take  $\eta = F_{\Delta,\ell}(\Delta)$ , we recover the statements in the main text:  $\delta_1' = \frac{2\eta + 2\delta_1}{1 + \eta}$  and  $\delta_2' = \frac{2\delta_2}{1 + \eta}$ .


$$U_{K_{\Delta,\ell}}^{(B)}(\ket{0}^{\otimes(a_B+1)} \otimes \ket{\phi}) = \underbrace{\left[\ket{0}^{\otimes(a_B+1)} \otimes (c_1  \ket{\vec{a}} - c_2(1-\delta_1')\ket{\vec{b}} + c_2\delta_2'\ket{\vec{b'}})\right]}_{\text{success}} + \underbrace{|c_2|\sqrt{2\delta_1'-\delta_1'^2-\delta_2'^2}\ket{{\perp}}}_{\text{failure}}\,.$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1050 -->

From the bound  $|\delta_1| \leq \eta$ , we have  $0 \leq \delta_1' \leq \frac{4\eta}{1+\eta}$ . From the bound  $\sqrt{\delta_1^2 + \delta_2^2}$  we have  $\delta_2' \leq \sqrt{\delta_1'(\frac{4\eta}{1+\eta} - \delta_1')}$ .

## C QLSS given norm estimate: detailed analysis

First, we provide more specific details on how Algorithm 1 is implemented by providing a quantum circuit. The full circuit is given in Figure 11.

Figure 11: Quantum circuit implementing Algorithm 1. The gate  $U_{K_{\Delta,\ell}}^{(G_t)}$  is the QSVT circuit depicted in Figure 9 that implements the polynomial  $K_{\Delta,\ell}$  on the singular values of  $G_t$ , leveraging the (1, a+1)-block-encoding of  $G_t$  from Figure 6. Vertical lines separate steps 1, 2, and 3 in the algorithm. Step 2 (KR) succeeds if all a+2 ancillas in the second register are measured in  $|0\rangle$ . Step 3 succeeds if the first ancilla is measured to be  $|0\rangle$ . Conditioned on success, the output is  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$ .

**Theorem 1.** Suppose that  $\|\mathbf{b}\| = 1$ , that  $\mathbf{b}$  is in the column space of A, and that all nonzero singular values of A lie in the interval  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ . Let  $U_A$  be a (1, a)-block-encoding of A, and  $U_b$  be a state-preparation unitary for  $|\mathbf{b}\rangle$ . Let  $\mathbf{x}$  denote the unique vector of minimum norm  $\|\mathbf{x}\|$  for which  $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ .

Fix parameter choices  $\eta > 0$ , and  $t \in [1, \kappa]$ , letting  $\theta_t = \arctan(\|\boldsymbol{x}\|/t)$ . Consider Algorithm 1, denoting the overall success probability by  $p_{\text{succ}}$ , and the output state conditioned on success by  $|\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle$ . Then, the state  $|\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle$  has no overlap with the kernel of the operator A. Furthermore, the following bounds are satisfied (the first three require  $\eta/\cos(\theta_t) \leq 1$ )

$$\||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle - |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\| \le \arcsin\left(\frac{\eta}{\cos(\theta_t)}\right) \tag{68}$$

$$\frac{1}{2} \||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{x}| - |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}|\|_1 \le \frac{\eta}{\cos(\theta_t)} \tag{69}$$


$$|\langle \tilde{\boldsymbol{x}} | \boldsymbol{x} \rangle| \ge \sqrt{1 - \frac{\eta^2}{\cos^2(\theta_t)}} \tag{70}$$

$$\sin^{2}(2\theta_{t}) \left(\frac{1-\eta}{1+\eta}\right)^{2} \le p_{\text{succ}} \le \sin^{2}(2\theta_{t}) + \frac{4\eta^{2}}{(1+\eta)^{2}} \sin^{2}(\theta_{t}) \tag{71}$$

## PDF page 23


The query cost of the protocol is  $\ell$  queries to controlled- $U_A$ ,  $\ell$  queries controlled- $U_A^{\dagger}$ ,  $2\ell$  queries to controlled- $U_b^{\dagger}$ , and  $2\ell$  queries to controlled- $U_b^{\dagger}$  where


$$\ell = \left\lceil \frac{\arccosh(\eta^{-1})}{\arccosh\left(\frac{1+\kappa^{-2}}{1-\kappa^{-2}}\right)} \right\rceil  \leq \left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta}) \right\rceil$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1089 -->

The algorithm requires a + 3 total ancilla qubits, including the a ancilla qubits for  $U_A$ .

*Proof.* The stated query cost follows from the observations in Appendix B. This cost is incurred entirely during Step 2 of the algorithm (KR).

Note that since x is defined as the solution to Ax = b with minimum Euclidean norm ||x||, we have that x is orthogonal to the kernel of A. If it were not, we could project x onto the orthogonal complement of the kernel and obtain a vector x' for which Ax' = b and  $||x'|| \le ||x||$ , leading to a contradiction.

We now justify the stated bounds by tracking the state of throughout the algorithm. Recall that  $|x_t\rangle = \sin(\theta_t)|x\rangle + \cos(\theta_t)|e_n\rangle$ . Define  $|y_t\rangle = -\cos(\theta_t)|x\rangle + \sin(\theta_t)|e_n\rangle$  orthogonal to  $|x_t\rangle$ . Note that  $|y_t\rangle$  is orthogonal to the kernel of A. In Step 1, we prepare  $|e_n\rangle$ , which decomposes as

$$\ket{\vec{e_n}} = \cos(\theta_t)\ket{\vec{x_t}} + \sin(\theta_t)\ket{\vec{y_t}} \tag{73}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1100 -->

In Step 2, we apply KR for the matrix  $G_t = Q_{b'}A_t$ , which preserves  $|x_t\rangle$  and approximately flips the sign on  $|y_t\rangle$ . That is, conditioned on success of KR, using Eq. (67), the resulting state is (up to normalization)

$$\cos(\theta_t)\ket{\vec{x_t}} - (1-\delta_1')\sin(\theta_t)\ket{\vec{y_t}} +  \delta_2' \sin(\theta_t)\ket{\vec{z}}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1104 -->

where  $|z\rangle$  is some state orthogonal to both  $|x_t\rangle$  and  $|y_t\rangle$ ,  $\delta_1' \leq 4\eta/(1+\eta)$ , and  $\delta_2' \leq \sqrt{\delta_1'(4\eta/(1+\eta)-\delta_1')}$ . Furthermore, since the kernel of A is contained in the kernel of  $G_t = Q_{b'}A_t$  and  $y_t$  is orthogonal to the kernel of A, the image of  $y_t$  under any QSVT sequence will remain orthogonal to the kernel of A. In particular, this implies that  $|z\rangle$  is orthogonal to the kernel of A.

In Step 3, we project onto the complement of  $|e_n\rangle$ . Note that  $|z\rangle$  is orthogonal to  $|e_n\rangle$  and to  $|x\rangle$ , both of which lie in the span of  $\{|x_t\rangle, |y_t\rangle\}$ . The state that results is (up to normalization)

$$\begin{aligned}\ket{\vec{\tilde{x}}} &\propto \Big[\sin(\theta_t)\cos(\theta_t) + (1-\delta_1')\cos(\theta_t)\sin(\theta_t)\Big]\ket{\vec{x}} +  \delta_2'\sin(\theta_t)\ket{\vec{z}} \\
        &=\left(1-\frac{\delta_1'}{2}\right)\sin(2\theta_t)\ket{\vec{x}} + \delta_2' \sin(\theta_t) \ket{\vec{z}}\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1110 -->
 (75)



This state is orthogonal to the kernel of A since both x and z are orthogonal to the kernel of A.

We verify the bounds in reverse order. The overall probability of success is given by the squared norm of the right-hand side of Eq. (76). The lower bound in Eq. (71) follows from the observation that the squared norm is minimized when  $\delta_1' = 4\eta/(1+\eta)$  and  $\delta_2' = 0$ . The upper bound in Eq. (71) is a loose upper bound generated by maximizing the norm of each term in Eq. (76) individually, without enforcing  $\delta_2'^2 \le \delta_1'(4\eta/(1+\eta) - \delta_1') \le 4\eta^2/(1+\eta)^2$ . These terms achieve maximum norm at  $\delta_1' = 0$  and  $\delta_2' = 2\eta/(1+\eta)$ , yielding the stated upper bound.

To show Eq. (70), to condense some notation, define  $M = \eta/(1+\eta)$ ,  $\xi_1 = \delta_1'/2 - M$ ,  $\xi_2 = \delta_2'/2$ , and  $\gamma = 1/\cos(\theta_t)$ . We have  $\xi_1^2 + \xi_2^2 \leq M^2$ . After normalizing  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$  appropriately, and noting that  $\delta_2' \sin(\theta_t) = \sin(2\theta_t)\gamma\xi_2$ , we see that the overlap is given by

$$\begin{aligned}\lvert \braket{\vec{\tilde{x}}}{\vec{x}}\rvert &= \frac{1-M-\xi_1}{\sqrt{(1-M-\xi_1)^2 + \gamma^2\xi_2^2}} \\
         &\geq \frac{1-M-\gamma\xi_1}{\sqrt{(1-M-\gamma\xi_1)^2 + \gamma^2\xi_2^2}}\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1121 -->
 (77)




where imposing the inequality puts the expression in a form that easier to minimize. The pair  $(\xi_1, \xi_2)$  where it is minimized will be among the points for which  $|\xi_1|^2 + |\xi_2|^2$  achieves its maximum of  $M^2$ , so we let  $\xi_1 = M \sin(\varphi)$ 

## PDF page 24


and  $\xi_2 = M \cos(\varphi)$  for some  $\varphi$  to be specified later. Our lower bound on the overlap simplifies to


$$\frac{1-M - M \gamma\sin(\varphi)}{\sqrt{(1-M-M\gamma\sin(\varphi))^2 + \gamma^2 M^2\cos(\varphi))^2}}
        %
\tag{79}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1126 -->

We can upper bound the final expression by a geometric argument, aided by Figure 12.

Figure 12: Geometric argument for rigorous lower bound on overlap, see text. The angle  $\angle BCD$  is essentially defined as  $\varphi$ . We then deduce that if C is constrained to lie on the circle, then the maximum value of  $\angle BAC$  is  $\varphi$  which is achieved when AC is tangent to the circle.

Let ABC be a triangle, with AB of length  $1-M-M\gamma\sin(\varphi)$ , BC of length  $\gamma M\cos(\varphi)$  and  $\angle ABC$  a right angle. Then the lower bound on the overlap is equal to the cosine of the angle  $\angle BAC$ . Extend the line segment AB to a point D for which the length of AD is 1-M and the length of BD is  $M\gamma\sin(\varphi)$ . Then the length of DC is  $M\gamma$ , independent of  $\varphi$ , and the locus of points C as  $\varphi$  varies is a circle of radius  $M\gamma$  centered at D. The angle  $\angle BAC$  is maximized when AC is tangent to this circle, in which case AC is perpendicular to DC. Noting similar triangles, we see that the maximum of  $\angle BAC$  is precisely  $\varphi$ , and in this case,  $\sin(\varphi)$  is  $M\gamma/(1-M)$ . This implies

$$\begin{aligned}\lvert \braket{\vec{\tilde{x}}}{\vec{x}}\rvert 
        \geq \sqrt{1-\frac{M^2\gamma^2}{(1-M)^2}} = \sqrt{1-\frac{\eta^2}{\cos^2(\theta_t)}}\,.\end{aligned} \tag{80}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1188 -->

This bound implies Eq. (69) by the relationship  $||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{x}| - |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}||_1 = 2\sqrt{1-|\langle\boldsymbol{x}|\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle|^2}$ . Finally, to show Eq. (68), we return to the geometric argument. We note that our method guarantees that the overlap  $\langle \tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle$  is a positive real number, and thus, the lower bound on the overlap  $\langle \tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle = \cos(\varphi)$  implies that the length  $||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle - |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle||$  is upper bounded by the angle  $\varphi = \arcsin(\eta/\cos(\theta_t))$ .

Corollary 1. Fix a known constant  $\beta \geq 1$  and a value  $t \in [1, \kappa]$ . Suppose  $\|\mathbf{x}\|$  satisfies the promise  $\|\mathbf{x}\| \in [\beta^{-1}t, \beta t]$  (the case where  $\|\mathbf{x}\|$  is known exactly corresponds to  $\beta = 1$ ). Then, the QLSP can be solved with an expected number Q of combined controlled queries to  $U_A$  or  $U_A^{\dagger}$ , and Q combined controlled queries to Q or Q. where

$$Q \leq \frac{(\beta^2+1)^2}{\beta^2}\left(\frac{1+\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{\beta^2+1}}}{1-\frac{\varepsilon}{2\sqrt{\beta^2+1}}}\right)^2\left\lceil\frac{\kappa}{2}\ln\left(\frac{4\sqrt{\beta^2+1}}{\varepsilon}\right)\right\rceil\,.$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1197 -->

Proof. As previously, let  $\theta_t = \arctan(\|\boldsymbol{x}\|/t)$ , implying  $\cos(\theta_t) = t/\sqrt{t^2 + \|\boldsymbol{x}\|^2} \ge 1/\sqrt{\beta^2 + 1}$  and  $\sin(2\theta_t) = 2t\|\boldsymbol{x}\|/(t^2 + \|\boldsymbol{x}\|^2) \ge 2\beta/(\beta^2 + 1)$ . Choose  $\eta = \varepsilon/(2\sqrt{\beta^2 + 1})$ , so that the distance in Eq. (69) is less than  $\varepsilon$ . Run Algorithm 1. The success probability is lower bounded in Theorem 1 by  $p_{\text{succ}} \ge [2\beta(1-\eta)]^2/[(\beta^2 + 1)(1+\eta)]^2$ . The protocol must be repeated an expected  $1/p_{\text{succ}}$  times to observe success, and the query complexity of each run of the protocol contributes a factor  $2\ell$  to Q, where  $\ell$  is given in Eq. (72).

## PDF page 25


## D Estimating the norm: detailed analysis

In Section 5.2 of the main text, we showed that it was possible to learn a 2-approximation to the norm with high probability in complexity  $O(\kappa \log \log(\kappa) \log \log \log(\kappa))$ . The method sketched there requires many repetitions at each step of the binary search, which would contribute unfavorably toward the constant prefactors and the practical performance. We still think the noisy binary search may be a promising practical method, but here we examine an alternative method with  $O(\kappa \log(\kappa) \log \log(\kappa))$  complexity that more closely resembles the exhaustive search method from Section 5.1. We then show how to reduce it to  $O(\kappa \sqrt{\log(\kappa)} \log \log(\kappa))$  with amplitude amplification. In both cases we give explicit expressions for the resource cost. Note that  $\sqrt{\log(\kappa)}$  is not much larger than  $\ln \ln(\kappa)$  for many practical values of  $\kappa$ . For example  $\ln(\ln(10^6)) = 2.6$ , whereas  $\sqrt{\ln(10^6)} = 3.7$ .

The idea—implemented in Algorithm 2—is to guess random values of t, and then run Algorithm 1 using that value of t. If it succeeds, output t, and if it fails, output fail. Choices of t that are closer to  $\|x\|$  will be output more often since they lead to larger values of  $p_{\text{succ}}$  for Algorithm 1 (see Figure 2). When it succeeds, Algorithm 2 also outputs a state  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$ . This might be regarded as a solution to the QLSP, but its complexity would have a term of the form  $O(\kappa \log(\kappa) \log(1/\varepsilon))$ . This can be avoided by instead viewing the output of Algorithm 2 as an ansatz state, and then refining it with KP. We analyze this two-step algorithm later in Algorithm 3.

In Algorithm 2, we are given as input an interval  $[\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}]$  that is known to contain  $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|$ —equivalently  $\ln(\|\boldsymbol{x}\|) \in [\ln(\mathcal{L}), \ln(\mathcal{R})]$ . We choose  $\tau$  essentially uniformly at random from the interval  $[\ln(\mathcal{L}), \ln(\mathcal{R})]$ , but we oversample the edge points  $\tau = \ln(\mathcal{L})$  and  $\tau = \ln(\mathcal{R})$ . The reason for this is related to the fact that when we draw  $\tau$  uniformly at random from the interval  $[\ln(\mathcal{L}), \ln(\mathcal{R})]$ , the average distance between  $\tau$  and  $\ln(\|\boldsymbol{x}\|)$  is larger when  $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|$  is near a boundary of the interval than when it is not near the boundary. By oversampling the edge points, we compensate for this effect.

**Algorithm 2:** Estimating the norm by randomly choosing t + postselection

Input:  $(A, b, [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}], \kappa, \eta)$ 

**Output:**  $(t, |\tilde{x}\rangle)$  with probability  $q_{\text{succ}}$ , and "fail" with probability  $1 - q_{\text{succ}}$ 

- 1 Choose  $\tau$  uniformly at random from  $\left[\ln(\mathcal{L}) \frac{1}{2}, \ln(\mathcal{R}) + \frac{1}{2}\right]$
- 2 if  $\tau \leq \ln(\mathcal{L})$ :
- 3 Set  $\tau = \ln(\mathcal{L})$
- 4 elif  $\tau \geq \ln(\mathcal{R})$ :
- $5 \mid \operatorname{Set} \tau = \ln(\mathcal{R})$
- 6 Set  $t = e^{\tau}$
- 7 Run Algorithm 1 with parameters  $(A, b, \kappa, \eta, t)$ . If it fails, output "fail." If it succeeds, denote its output by  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$
- s return  $(t, |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle)$

**Lemma 4.** Let  $\eta > 0$ ,  $\mathcal{L}$ , and  $\mathcal{R}$  be fixed and known parameters and suppose  $\|\mathbf{x}\| \in [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}]$ . Let  $q_{\text{succ}}$  denote the success probability of Algorithm 2 on parameters  $(A, \mathbf{b}, [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}], \kappa, \eta)$ , and when it succeeds denote its output by  $(t, |\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\rangle)$ . The probability of success satisfies


$$q_{\rm succ} \geq \frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)} \tag{82}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1236 -->

and the query cost of the algorithm is the same as that of Theorem 1. Postselecting on success, for any  $\beta$  the output t satisfies

$$\begin{aligned}\Pr\left[t \not\in [\beta^{-1}\nrm{\vec{x}}, \beta \nrm{\vec{x}}] \right]
            \leq{} \frac{(1+\eta)^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\frac{4}{\beta^2+1} + \frac{2\eta^2(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1-4\ln(\beta))}{(1-\eta)^2}\,.\end{aligned} \tag{83}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1241 -->

When it succeeds it also outputs the same  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$  that appears in Theorem 1 for the corresponding value of t and  $\eta$ . Denoting the ensemble of  $|\tilde{x}\rangle\langle\tilde{x}|$  by the mixed state  $\tilde{\rho}$ , we have

$$\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}\ket{\vec{x}} \geq 1- \frac{2\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right) \right) \tag{84}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1246 -->

*Proof.* Observe that Algorithm 2 chooses  $\tau$  randomly as follows. With probability  $1/(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 2)$  it chooses  $\tau = \ln(\mathcal{L})$ , with probability  $1/(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 2)$  it chooses  $\tau = \ln(\mathcal{R})$ , and with probability  $\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})/(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 1)$ , it

## PDF page 26


chooses  $\tau$  uniformly at random from the interval  $[\ln(\mathcal{L}), \ln(\mathcal{R})]$ . It then runs Algorithm 1 (analyzed in Theorem 1) using  $t = e^{\tau}$ , and if it succeeds, outputs t and the resulting state  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$ . Since Algorithm 1 is called exactly one time, the query cost is identical to that of Algorithm 1.

Let  $Q_t$  denote the probability that Algorithm 1 succeeds for a certain choice of t, which can be lower bounded by Eq. (71). The overall probability of success is at least

$$\begin{aligned}q_{\rm succ} &= \frac{1}{2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2} \left(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{L}} + \mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{R}} +2\int_{\ln(\mathcal{L})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \mathcal{Q}_t\right) \end{aligned} \tag{85}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1254 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        &\geq \frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2)} \left(\sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) + \sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) +2\int_{\ln(\mathcal{L})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \sin^2(2\theta_t)\right)\end{aligned} \tag{86}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1254 -->

where  $\theta_t = \arctan(\|\boldsymbol{x}\|/e^{\tau})$ . Computing  $d\theta_t/d\tau = -\sin(2\theta_t)/2$ , we make a change of integration variable and express the lower bound as

$$q_{\text{succ}} \ge \frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2 (2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 2)} \left( \sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) + \sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) + 4 \int_{\theta_{\mathcal{R}}}^{\theta_{\mathcal{L}}} d\theta_t \sin(2\theta_t) \right) \tag{87}$$

$$= \frac{(1-\eta)^2(\sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) + \sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) + 2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) - 2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}))}{(1+\eta)^2(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 2)} \tag{88}$$

$$=\frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)}$$


$$+\frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2)}\left[(2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}})-\cos^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}))+(-2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}})-\cos^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}})\right] \tag{89}$$

$$\geq \frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)} \tag{90}$$

The last line follows by noting the following two facts. First, since  $\mathcal{L} \leq ||x||$ , we have  $-1 \leq \cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) \leq 0$  and hence  $-2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) - \cos^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) \geq 0$ . Second, since  $\mathcal{R} \geq ||x||$ , we have  $0 \leq \cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) \leq 1$  and hence  $2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) - \cos^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) \geq 0$ . This confirms Eq. (82).

We can now compute an upper bound on the probability that the algorithm succeeds and outputs an estimate for t greater than  $\beta \|\mathbf{x}\|$ , which we denote by  $P_>$ . For this to be the case, first of all, the random choice of  $\tau$  must be greater than  $\beta \|\mathbf{x}\|$ . Then, conditioned on such a choice of  $\tau$ , the probability of success  $Q_t$  is upper bounded by Eq. (71). If  $\|\mathbf{x}\| < \mathcal{R}/\beta$ , we have

$$\begin{aligned}P_{>} &= \frac{1}{2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2}\left(\mathcal{Q}_{\mathcal{R}} + 2\int_{\ln(\beta \nrm{\vec{x}})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \mathcal{Q}_t \right)\end{aligned} \tag{91}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1269 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        &\leq \frac{1}{2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2}\left( 2\int_{\ln(\beta \nrm{\vec{x}})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \left(\sin^2(2\theta_t)+\frac{4\eta^2\sin^2(\theta_t)}{(1+\eta)^2}\right) + \sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) +\frac{4\eta^2\sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{R}})}{(1+\eta)^2}\right)\end{aligned} \tag{92}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1269 -->

and if  $||x|| \ge b/\beta$ , we have  $P_> = 0$ . Following the method that arrived at Eq. (90), we compute

$$2\int_{\ln(\beta \nrm{\vec{x}})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \sin^2(2\theta_t) = 2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) - 2\cos(2\theta_{\beta\nrm{\vec{x}}}) \,.$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1274 -->

and we note that  $2\cos(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) + \sin^2(2\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) = 2 - 4\sin^4(\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) \le 2$ . We also note that  $2 - 2\cos(\theta_{\beta||\boldsymbol{x}||}) = 4\sin^2(\theta_{\beta||\boldsymbol{x}||}) = \frac{4}{\beta^2 + 1}$ .

$$\begin{aligned}P_{>} &\leq \frac{1}{2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2}
        \left(\frac{4}{\beta^2+1} + \frac{4\eta^2}{(1+\eta)^2}\left(
        \sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) + 2\int_{\ln(\beta\nrm{\vec{x}})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d \tau \sin^2(\theta_t)
        \right)
        \right)\end{aligned} \tag{94}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1278 -->

Similarly, we can compute the probability that the algorithm succeeds and outputs a value of  $t < ||x||/\beta$ . If  $||x|| \le \beta \mathcal{L}$ , then  $P_{<} = 0$ . If  $||x|| > \beta \mathcal{L}$ , we follow the same process as above, and we arrive at

$$\begin{aligned}P_{<} &\leq \frac{1}{2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2}
        \left(\frac{4}{\beta^2+1} + \frac{4\eta^2}{(1+\eta)^2}\left(
        \sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) + 2\int_{\ln(\mathcal{L})}^{\ln(\nrm{\vec{x}}/\beta)} d \tau \sin^2(\theta_t)
        \right)
        \right)\end{aligned} \tag{95}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1286 -->

We will add these two expressions to get an upper bound on the overall probability that the output deviates by a factor  $\beta$ . To bound the  $O(\eta^2)$  terms under the integral, we simply assert that  $\sin^2(\theta_t) \leq 1$ . First, suppose that  $\mathcal{L}\beta \leq ||x|| \leq \mathcal{R}/\beta$ . This implies that both  $P_{<}$  and  $P_{>}$  are nonzero, and also that  $\sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) \leq 1/(\beta^2 + 1)$  and  $\sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) \leq \beta^2/(\beta^2 + 1)$ , so  $\sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{L}}) + \sin^2(\theta_{\mathcal{R}}) \leq 1$ . We find that

<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1294 -->

If  $\mathcal{L}\beta \leq ||x|| \leq \mathcal{R}/\beta$  does not hold, then this implies that at least one of  $P_{<}$  and  $P_{>}$  is zero and it is easy to see that the above expression still holds.

<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1307 -->

$$\begin{aligned}

<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1316 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        &\leq \frac{2\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right) \right)\end{aligned} \tag{102}$$

Briefly, we now discuss how this theorem enables us to learn the norm up to a constant approximation ratio. Suppose  $\beta$  is a fixed constant greater than 3. We may take  $\eta \geq 0.1(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 1 - 4\ln(\beta))^{-1/2} \geq \Omega(\kappa^{-1/2})$  so that the right-hand-side of Eq. (83) is upper bounded by 0.45. Thus, there is a strictly greater than 1/2 chance the output of Algorithm 2 is a 3-approximation of  $\|\mathbf{x}\|$ . The expected number of times we need to run Algorithm 2 to observe success is upper bounded by  $q_{\text{succ}}^{-1} \approx \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) + 1 \leq \ln(\kappa) + 1$ . Thus, the overall complexity to learn a 3-approximation to the norm is  $O(\kappa \log(\kappa) \log \log(\kappa))$ . The probability the output is a 3-approximation can be boosted to  $1 - \delta$  with median amplification at the cost of multiplicative  $O(\log(1/\delta))$  overhead. The approximation ratio can be improved from 3 to  $1 + \varepsilon$  using the method from Section 5.4.

## PDF page 28


Now we describe how the  $O(\log(\kappa))$  complexity coming from the required number of repetitions can be improved to  $O(\sqrt{\log(\kappa)})$  with fixed-point amplitude amplification.

**Lemma 5.** Let  $\eta > 0$ ,  $\delta > 0$ ,  $\mathcal{L}$ , and  $\mathcal{R}$  be fixed constants and suppose  $\|\mathbf{x}\| \in [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}]$ , and let d be a positive integer. Suppose we restrict values of t such that  $\ln(t) - \mathcal{L}$  an integer multiples of  $2^{-d}$ , and we run Algorithm 2 coherently by creating a superposition over a set of discrete t values, rather than a classical random guess. Then, we may wrap Algorithm 2 in fixed-point amplitude amplification, and boost its probability of success to  $1 - \delta$ . The output  $(t, \tilde{\mathbf{x}})$  of this protocol is identical to that of Algorithm 2 up to discretization error of order  $2^{-d}$ . Specifically

$$\begin{aligned}\Pr\left[t \not\in [\beta^{-1}\nrm{\vec{x}}, \beta \nrm{\vec{x}}] \right]
            \leq{}& \frac{(1+\eta)^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\frac{4}{\beta^2+1} +\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^2 2^{-d-1} +  \frac{2\eta^2(2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1-4\ln(\beta))}{(1-\eta)^2}\\
            %
            \bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}\ket{\vec{x}} \geq{}& 1- \frac{2\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right) + \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d} \right)\left(1-\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d-1}\right)^{-1} \,.
            \end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1330 -->
(103)



The query cost of the algorithm is Q total queries to  $U_A$ ,  $U_A^{\dagger}$  and their controlled versions, and 2Q total queries to  $U_b$ ,  $U_b^{\dagger}$ , and their controlled versions, where

$$Q \leq \left(2\left\lceil \frac{(1+\eta)\sqrt{\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1}}{2(1-\eta)} \left(1-\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d-1}\right)^{-1/2} \ln(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\delta}})-\frac{1}{2} \right\rceil +1\right)2\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta}) \right\rceil \tag{105}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1338 -->

The algorithm requires  $d + \lceil \log_2 \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) \rceil$  additional ancilla qubits, compared to Algorithm 2.

*Proof.* First, we describe in more detail the modifications that allow fixed-point amplitude amplification to be applied. Let  $q = d + \lceil \log_2 \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) \rceil + 1$  be the number of ancilla qubits. Let  $j_{\text{max}} = \lceil \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L}) 2^d \rceil \le 2^{q-1}$ . We require a unitary  $\mathcal{P}$  that prepares the state

$$\mathcal{P}\ket{0}\ket{0^{q}} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2 \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+2}}\left(\ket{0}\ket{0^q}+\ket{0}\ket{10^{q-1}} + \frac{\sqrt{2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})}}{\sqrt{j_{\max}}}\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\max}-1}\ket{1}\ket{j} \right) \tag{106}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1345 -->

We require another unitary  $\mathcal{B}$  which applies the block-encoding  $U_{A_t}$  for matrix  $A_t$ , where the value of t is controlled by the setting of the q+1 qubits depicted in the equation above. Referencing Figure 4, to control the value of t, we merely need to control the value of the rotation angle in the block-encoding  $U_{A_t}$ . Let  $t_j = e^{a+j2^{-d}}$ , so that  $\ln(t_j)$ is the left edgepoint of the interval  $[a+j2^{-d}, a+(j+1)2^{-d}]$ . Then, we require that

$$\mathcal{B} = \ketbra{0^{q+1}} \otimes U_{A_\mathcal{L}} + \ketbra{010^{q-1}} \otimes U_{A_{\mathcal{R}}} + \sum_{j=0}^{2^d-1} \ketbra{1j} \otimes U_{A_{t_j}} \tag{107}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1349 -->

Then, we may run Algorithm 2 by using  $\mathcal{P}$  to generate the random distribution of values of t, and replace each occurrence of  $U_{At}$  by  $\mathcal{B}$ . The analysis of the algorithm is the same in Lemma 4, except that the integrals are approximated by discrete sums. To bound the error in this approximation, we invoke the well-known formula that the difference between an integral on the interval [L,R] and a sum of the integrand evaluated at the left edge point of N equally sized subintervals is at most  $K_1(R-L)^2/(2N)$ , where  $K_1$  is an upper bound on the derivative of the integrand on the interval. We note the (non-optimized) upper bounds

$$\begin{aligned}\left\lvert \frac{d}{d\tau}(\sin^2(2\theta_t))\right\rvert  = \left\lvert - \sin(2\theta_t)\sin(4\theta_t)/2\right\rvert &\leq 1/2 \\
        \left\lvert \frac{d}{d\tau}(\sin^2(\theta_t))\right\rvert  = \left\lvert - \sin^2(2\theta_t)\right\rvert &\leq 1 \\\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1353 -->



(110)

## PDF page 29


The approximation errors satisfy

$$\begin{aligned}\left\lvert \int_{\ln(\mathcal{L})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \sin^2(2\theta_t) - 2^{-d}\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\max-1}} \sin^2(2\theta_{t_j})\right\rvert &\leq \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^2 2^{-d-2} \end{aligned} \tag{111}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1358 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        \left\lvert \int_{\ln(\mathcal{L})}^{\ln(\mathcal{R})} d\tau \sin^2(\theta_t) - 2^{-d}\sum_{j=0}^{j_{\max-1}} \sin^2(\theta_{t_j})\right\rvert &\leq \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^2 2^{-d-1}\end{aligned} \tag{112}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1358 -->

These integrals are used in Lemma 4 in Eqs. (90) and (100). Thus, we instead obtain

$$\begin{aligned}q_{\rm succ} &\geq  \frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)} \left(1-\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d-1}\right) \end{aligned} \tag{113}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1363 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
        1-\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}\ket{\vec{x}} 
        &\leq \frac{2\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right) + \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d} \right)\left(1-\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d-1}\right)^{-1}\end{aligned} \tag{114}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1363 -->

Through a similar method, we also obtain

$$P_> + P_< \leq \frac{1}{\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1}\left(\frac{4}{\beta^2+1} + \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^2 2^{-d-1} + \frac{2\eta^2}{(1+\eta)^2}\left(1+2\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})-4\ln(\beta))\right)\right)\,.$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1369 -->

Using this coherent version of Algorithm 3, we can wrap the algorithm in fixed-point amplitude amplification [24]. Given a lower bound  $\lambda$  on the success probability (as in Eq. (113)), fixed-point amplitude amplification allows the success probability to be boosted to at least  $1 - \delta$  with using L calls to the algorithm, where L is the smallest odd integer greater than  $\lambda^{-1/2} \ln \left( 2/\sqrt{\delta} \right)$ , and an equal number of calls to the oracle that determines success. Here, determining success is simply checking whether the ancilla qubits are  $|0\rangle$ . Thus, the overall query complexity is the number L times the query complexity of Algorithm 1, yielding the quoted statement.

## E Explicit complexity bounds for QLSS

#### E.1 Near-optimal QLSSs

Now, we present a full QLSS that does not require knowing an estimate for the norm ahead of time. All it needs to know is that  $\|x\| \in [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}]$  (we can always take  $[\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}] = [1, \kappa]$  if we have no additional information). In this subsection, we do not yet implement the adiabatic-inspired ideas described in Section 5.3 to achieve linear-in- $\kappa$  complexity; we so so in Section E.2. The algorithm presented here is a randomized algorithm that outputs an ensemble of pairs  $(t, |\tilde{x}\rangle)$ , where t is an estimate for  $\|x\|$  and  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$  is the associated output of Algorithm 1 for that choice of t. It works by running Algorithm 2 to generate a good norm estimate t and associated anstatz state  $|x_{\rm ans}\rangle$ , and then using KP to refine that state. The KP step should be run with precision


$$\eta_{\rm KP} = \frac{\varepsilon}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{1-\mu^2}}{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2}}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1381 -->

where

$$\mu = \frac{\eta}{1-\eta}\sqrt{3 + 2\ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right)} \tag{117}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1385 -->

We show that the ensemble  $\tilde{\rho}$  of  $|\tilde{x}\rangle\langle\tilde{x}|$  that the algorithm outputs is  $\varepsilon$ -close to  $|x\rangle\langle x|$ .

**Theorem 2.** Suppose that  $\|\mathbf{b}\| = 1$ , that  $\mathbf{b}$  is in the column space of A, and that all nonzero singular values of A lie in the interval  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ . Let  $U_A$  be a (1, a)-block-encoding of A, and  $U_{\mathbf{b}}$  be a state-preparation unitary for  $|\mathbf{b}\rangle$ . Let  $\mathbf{x}$  denote the unique vector of minimum norm  $\|\mathbf{x}\|$  for which  $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ . Fix  $\eta > 0$  and  $\varepsilon > 0$ , and assume  $\eta$  satisfies  $\eta/(1-\eta) < (3+2\ln((\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2)/(2\mathcal{L})^2))^{-1/2}$ . Let  $\eta_{\mathrm{KP}} > 0$  be given by Eq. (116). Consider Algorithm 3, denoting its output by  $(t, |\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\rangle)$ .

Then, the ensemble of outputs  $|\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}|$ , denoted by  $\tilde{\rho}$ , satisfies  $\frac{1}{2}|||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{x}| - \tilde{\rho}||_1 \leq \varepsilon$ , meaning the output solves the QLSP. Furthermore, the expected number of queries required is Q total queries to  $U_A$ ,  $U_A^{\dagger}$  and their controlled

## PDF page 30


#### **Algorithm 3:** QLSS using random choice of t + postselection + KP

Input:  $(A, \boldsymbol{b}, [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}], \kappa, \eta, \varepsilon)$ 

Output:  $(t, |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle)$ 

- 1 Let  $\eta_{\rm KP}$  be given by Eq. (116).
- **2** Run Algorithm 2 with parameters  $(A, \boldsymbol{b}, [\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}], \kappa, \eta)$ . If it fails, repeat this step. If it succeeds, denote its output by  $(t, |\boldsymbol{x}_{ans}\rangle)$
- **3** Apply KP to  $|x_{ans}\rangle$  using the matrix  $G = Q_b A$  with parameters  $(\kappa, \eta_{KP})$ . If it fails, go to line 2. If it succeeds, denote its output by  $|\tilde{x}\rangle$ .
- 4 return  $(t, |\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle)$

versions, as well as 2Q total queries to  $U_{\bf b}$ ,  $U_{\bf b}^{\dagger}$ , and their controlled versions, where

$$\begin{aligned}Q \leq \frac{2(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)}{(1-\eta)^2}\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta}) \right\rceil + \frac{1}{1-\frac{\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(3 + 2\ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right)\right)}2\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta_{\rm KP}}) \right\rceil\end{aligned} \tag{118}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1408 -->

*Proof.* First, we verify that the output solves the QLSP. From Lemma 4, the ensemble of outputs of Algorithm 2, denoted by  $\tilde{\rho}_{ans}$ , satisfies

$$\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}_{\rm ans}\ket{\vec{x}} \geq 1- \frac{2\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right) \right) \tag{119}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1415 -->

The subsequent KP step can then be analyzed with Lemma 2, with


$$\mu = \frac{\eta}{1-\eta}\sqrt{3 + 2\ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right)} \tag{120}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1419 -->

It asserts that the output of the KP step satisfies

$$\frac{1}{2}\nrm{\ketbra{\vec{x}}-\tilde{\rho}}_1 \leq \frac{\mu\eta_{\rm KP}}{\sqrt{1-\mu^2+\mu^2\eta_{\rm KP}^2}} = \varepsilon\,. \tag{121}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1423 -->

where the last equality is ensured by the choice of  $\eta_{\rm KP}$  in Eq. (116)

Next we verify the expected query complexity. We examine the expected complexity from each of the two lines separately. For line 2, each time it is called, it has the complexity quoted in Theorem 1. The expected number of times it is called is  $r_{\text{succ}}^{-1}$ , where  $r_{\text{succ}}$  is the probability that both lines line 2 and line 3 succeed. We can see that this satisfies the same lower bound as  $q_{\text{succ}}$  from Eq. (90), since this lower bound comes entirely from the portion of the state that is left invariant by the KP step.

$$r_{\rm succ} \geq \frac{(1-\eta)^2}{(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)}\,. \tag{122}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1429 -->

Thus, the contribution to the expected query complexity from line 2 is at most

$$\frac{(1+\eta)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1)}{(1-\eta)^2}2\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta}) \right\rceil \tag{123}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1433 -->

On the other hand, line 3 only executes when line 2 succeeds, and the algorithm terminates immediately once line 3 succeeds for the first time. By Lemma 2, the success probability of line 3 is at least  $1 - \mu^2$ . Thus, the expected number of times line 3 executes is at most  $(1 - \mu^2)^{-1}$ , and its contribution to the query complexity is upper bounded by

$$\frac{2\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta_{\rm KP}}) \right\rceil}{1-\frac{\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(3 + 2\ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right)\right)}\,. \tag{124}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1438 -->

This completes the proof.

If one takes  $[\mathcal{L}, \mathcal{R}] = [1, \kappa]$  and  $\eta = \Theta(1/\log(\kappa))$ , then  $\mu = \Theta(1)$  and  $\eta_{KP} = \Theta(\varepsilon)$ . The overall query complexity is  $O(\kappa \log(\kappa) \log \log(\kappa) + \kappa \log(1/\varepsilon))$ . Furthermore, we may perform a closer inspection to derive a simpler formula

## PDF page 31


that holds over a wide range of values of  $\kappa$ . Suppose that  $\kappa \in [3, 10^6]$ , so that  $\sqrt{3 + 2 \ln((\kappa^2 + 1)/2)} \in [2.49, 7.55]$ . Choose  $\eta$  so that  $\mu = 0.25$ . This implies

$$\begin{aligned}\left(\frac{1+\eta}{1-\eta}\right)^2 = \left(1+\frac{2\eta}{1-\eta}\right)^2 = \left(1+0.5/\sqrt{3+2\ln((\kappa^2+1)/2)}\right)^2 \leq 1.442\,.\end{aligned} \tag{125}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1445 -->

Furthermore

$$\begin{aligned}\ln(2/\eta) = \ln(2/\mu) + \ln(\sqrt{3+2\ln((\kappa^2+1)/2)})+ \ln(1+\mu/\sqrt{3+2\ln((\kappa^2+1)/2)}) \leq  4.14\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1449 -->

Then, working from Eq. (118), we have

$$\begin{aligned}Q &\leq 2\cdot 1.442(\ln(\kappa)+1)\lceil (4.14)\frac{\kappa}{2}\rceil + \frac{16}{15}\cdot 2 \cdot \lceil\frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2})-\frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\sqrt{15}/2) \rceil\end{aligned} \tag{127}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1453 -->

Noting that  $\lceil x \rceil \leq x + 1$ , we can then write


$$\begin{aligned}Q &\leq 5.97 \ln(\kappa)\kappa +5.27\kappa + 1.07 \kappa \ln(\varepsilon^{-1}\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2}) + 2.89\ln(\kappa) + 5.02\,.\end{aligned} \tag{128}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1457 -->

This will be a loose bound compared to Eq. (118), especially for smaller  $\kappa$ , but it is a genuine upper bound that holds whenever  $\kappa \in [3, 10^6]$ .

We can reduce the prefactor on the first term by a quantity  $\sqrt{\log(\kappa)}$  by using (fixed-point) amplitude amplification to boost the chances of successfully preparing  $\rho_{\rm ans}$ , prior to performing KP.

**Theorem 3.** Consider Algorithm 3, with line 2 replaced by the fixed-point amplitude amplified version described in Lemma 5 for a certain amplification parameter  $\delta$ , and discretization parameter d. Then one can achieve the same results with

$$\begin{aligned}Q ={}& \frac{\frac{1}{1-\delta}\left(2\left\lceil \frac{(1+\eta)\sqrt{\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})+1}}{2(1-\eta)} \left(1-\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d-1}\right)^{-1/2} \ln(\frac{2}{\sqrt{\delta}})-\frac{1}{2} \right\rceil +1\right)2\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta}) \right\rceil+2\left\lceil \frac{\kappa}{2}\ln(\frac{2}{\eta_{\rm KP}}) \right\rceil}{1-\frac{\eta^2}{(1-\eta)^2}\left(3 + 2\ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}^2+\mathcal{L}^2}{2\mathcal{L}^2}\right) + \ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d+1} \right)\left(1-\ln(\mathcal{R}/\mathcal{L})^22^{-d-1}\right)^{-1} }\end{aligned} \tag{129}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1468 -->

*Proof.* This follows by following the same analysis as in Theorem 1, except using Lemma 5 in place of Lemma 4. The probability that both line 2 and line 3 succeed is at least  $(1 - \delta)(1 - \mu^2)$ , so the expected number of times the line 2 must run is at most the inverse of this quantity. As before line 3 must be repeated an expected number  $(1 - \mu^2)^{-1}$  times, leading to the quoted expression.

#### E.2 Optimal QLSS

In this subsection, we provide a rigorous and explicit version of the full QLSS with optimal dependence on  $\kappa$ , following a similar idea as that presented in Section 5.3. The algorithm leverages the family of matrices  $\bar{A}_{\sigma}$  defined in Eq. (33) to estimate  $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|$ . The algorithm is described in Algorithm 4, and we give explicit bounds on the expected query complexity in Theorem 4.

The algorithm takes in the QLSP parameters  $A, b, \kappa, \varepsilon$ . It also chooses a few additional free parameters,  $\hat{c}, \hat{q}, \hat{\beta}, \hat{r}, \hat{\chi}, \hat{\Delta}$ ; we denote these free parameters with hats for easy reference. These are independent of  $\kappa$  and  $\varepsilon$ , and they must satisfy  $\hat{c} > 1$ ,  $\hat{q} > 1$ ,  $\hat{\beta} > 1$ ,  $0 < \hat{\chi} < 1$ , and  $0 < \hat{\Delta} < 1$ . We give an expression for the query complexity

## PDF page 32


that depends on these parameters. A numerical optimization of this expression suggests a locally optimal choice for the parameters that we report in Table 2.

| param          | value   | role                                                                            |
|----------------|---------|---------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| $\hat{\beta}$  | 15.4    | target approximation ratio for $\ x\ $ prior to final step                      |
| $\hat{\chi}$   | 0.0398  | magnitude of errors in kernel reflection on final step                          |
| $\hat{c}$      | 20.0    | factor by which effective condition number $\sigma^{-1}$ is increased each step |
| $\hat{r}$      | 3.37    | factor by which target approximation ratio decreases each step                  |
| $\hat{q}$      | 5.41    | factor by which kernel reflection error parameter is increased each step        |
| $\hat{\Delta}$ | 0.00424 | upper bound on total probability of repeated failure of kernel reflection       |

Table 2: Summary of free parameters in Algorithm 4, along with their role. A specific choice for each parameter is provided which leads the final complexity statement to achieve a local minimum.

Additionally, in its operation and analysis, we define the following parameters to depend on the above. The index j ranges from 1 to J, where J is defined in the first line.


$$J = \lceil \ln(\kappa) / \ln(\hat{c}) \rceil$$

$$\sigma_{j} = \kappa^{-1} \hat{c}^{J-j} \in [\kappa^{-1}, 1]$$

$$\beta_{j} = \hat{\beta} \hat{r}^{J-j-1}$$

$$\chi_{j} = \hat{\chi} \hat{q}^{-J+j}$$

$$\eta_{j} = \chi_{j} / (1 + \chi_{j})$$

$$m_{j} = (J - j + 1) \ln(\hat{\Delta}^{-1} + 1) \frac{(1 + \eta_{j})^{2} (\ln(\hat{c} \hat{\beta}^{2} \hat{r}^{2J-2j}) + 1)}{(1 - \eta_{j})^{2}}$$

$$\bar{P} = \frac{4}{\hat{\beta}^{2} (1 - \hat{r}^{-2})} + \frac{16\hat{\chi}}{\hat{q} \hat{\beta}^{2} (1 - \hat{r}^{-2} \hat{q}^{-1})} + \frac{16\hat{\chi}^{2}}{\hat{q}^{2} \hat{\beta}^{2} (1 - \hat{r}^{-2} \hat{q}^{-2})} + \frac{2\hat{\chi}^{2} \ln(\hat{c} \hat{c}^{2} \hat{r}^{4})}{\hat{q}^{2} - 1}$$

$$\mu = \sqrt{1 - (1 - \hat{\Delta} - \bar{P}) \left(1 - 2\hat{\chi}^{2} \left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\hat{c} \hat{\beta}^{2} + 1}{2}\right)\right)\right)}$$

$$\eta_{KP} = \frac{\varepsilon}{\mu} \frac{\sqrt{1 - \mu^{2}}}{\sqrt{1 - \varepsilon^{2}}} \tag{132}$$

**Theorem 4.** Suppose that  $\|\mathbf{b}\| = 1$ , that  $\mathbf{b}$  is in the column space of A, and that all nonzero singular values of A lie in the interval  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ . Let  $U_A$  be a (1, a)-block-encoding of A, and  $U_b$  be a state-preparation unitary for  $|\mathbf{b}\rangle$ . Let  $\mathbf{x}$  denote the unique vector of minimum norm  $\|\mathbf{x}\|$  for which  $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ . Fix  $\varepsilon > 0$ . Consider Algorithm 4, denoting its output by  $(t, |\tilde{\mathbf{x}}\rangle)$ , using the parameter choices listed in Table 2.

Then, the ensemble of outputs  $|\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}\rangle\langle\tilde{\boldsymbol{x}}|$ , denoted by  $\tilde{\rho}$ , satisfies  $\frac{1}{2}|||\boldsymbol{x}\rangle\langle\boldsymbol{x}| - \tilde{\rho}||_1 \leq \varepsilon$ , meaning the output solves the QLSP. Furthermore, the expected number of queries required is Q total queries to  $U_A$ ,  $U_A^{\dagger}$  and their controlled versions, as well as 2Q total queries to  $U_b$ ,  $U_b^{\dagger}$ , and their controlled versions, where

$$\begin{aligned}Q \leq 56.0 \kappa + 1.05 \kappa \ln(\frac{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2}}{\varepsilon})  + 2.78 \ln(\kappa)^3 + 3.17\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1559 -->

*Proof.* The first part of the algorithm consists of computing a sequence of estimates  $t_j$  for  $j = 1, ..., J = \lceil \ln(\kappa) / \ln(\hat{c}) \rceil$ . The final step of this sequence also produces a state  $|x_j\rangle$ . For any value of j, we say that step j "succeeds" if the algorithm advances to step j + 1, and step j "fails" if all  $m_j$  repetitions within line 7 fail, causing

- Let  $[\mathcal{L}_j, \mathcal{R}_j] = [\max(1, t_{j-1}/\beta_{j-1}), \min(\sigma_j^{-1}, \hat{c}t_{j-1}\beta_{j-1})]$

If it fails, repeat this step up to  $m_j$  times. If all  $m_j$  repetitions fail, return to line 1. Upon first success, denote its output by  $(t_j, |\boldsymbol{x}_j\rangle)$  and continue.
- **8** Let  $\eta_{\rm KP}$  be given as in Eq. (132)

the algorithm to restart and return to line 1. We say that the algorithm begins a new "cycle" when one of the steps fails and it returns to line 1, with each cycle being completely independent of the previous cycles.

Conditioned on steps 1, 2, ..., j all succeeding, there is some fixed ensemble of outputs  $t_j$ . We argue that this ensemble is concentrated near  $\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_j}\|$  for all j. In particular, we will call an estimate  $t_j$  "good" if  $t_j \in [\beta_j^{-1}\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_j}\|, \beta_j\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\boldsymbol{\sigma}_j}\|]$  and "bad" otherwise. Furthermore, we recall from Property 3 shown in Section 5.3 that

$$\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma_{j}}}} \leq \nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma_{j+1}}}} \leq \nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma_{j}}}} \sigma_{j} / \sigma_{j+1} = \hat{c}\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma_{j-1}}}} \tag{134}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1568 -->

Thus, if  $t_j$  is good, it holds that  $\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sigma_{j+1}}\| \in [\beta_j^{-1}t_j, \beta_j\hat{c}t_j]$ . Separately we know that  $\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sigma_{j+1}}\| \in [1, \sigma_{j+1}^{-1}]$  since  $\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sigma_{j+1}}$  is the solution to a linear system with condition number at most  $\sigma_{j+1}^{-1}$ , so we conclude that  $\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sigma_{j+1}}\| \in [\beta_j^{-1}t_j, \beta_j\hat{c}t_j] \cap [1, \sigma_{j+1}^{-1}] = [\mathcal{L}_{j+1}, \mathcal{R}_{j+1}]$ .

Let  $p_j$  denote the probability that  $t_j$  is bad conditioned on  $\|\bar{x}_{\sigma_j}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_j, \mathcal{R}_j]$ , and note that by Lemma 4, we can bound  $p_j$  by


$$p_j := \Pr[t_j \text{ is bad}\;\; \big|\;\; \nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma_j}}} \in [\mathcal{L}_j, \mathcal{R}_j] ] \leq \frac{(1+\eta_j)^2}{(1-\eta_j)^2}\frac{4}{\beta_j^2+1} + \frac{2\eta_j^2(2\ln(\mathcal{R}_j/\mathcal{L}_j)+1-4\ln(\beta_j))}{(1-\eta_j)^2}\,. \tag{135}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1574 -->

Moreover, let  $\delta_j$  be the probability that all  $m_j$  repetitions of Algorithm 2 within step j fail, causing the algorithm to return to line 1, conditioned on  $\|\bar{x}_{\sigma_j}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_j, \mathcal{R}_j]$ . We know from Lemma 4 that the probability of success of each run of Algorithm 2 is at least  $q_{\text{succ}}$  where  $q_{\text{succ}}$  is bounded in Eq. (82), and thus  $\delta_j \leq (1 - q_{\text{succ}})^{m_j}$ . That is,

$$\begin{aligned}\delta_j \leq \left(1-\frac{(1-\eta_j)^2}{(1+\eta_j)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}_j/\mathcal{L}_j)+1)}\right)^{m_j}\,.\end{aligned} \tag{136}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1579 -->

Define


$$\begin{aligned}P &= \sum_{j=1}^{J-1} p_j \\
        \Delta &= \sum_{j=1}^{J} \delta_j\,.\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1584 -->



If the relation  $\|\bar{x}_{\sigma_j}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_j, \mathcal{R}_j]$  is satisfied, then step j will succeed and output a  $t_j$  that is good, except with probability at most  $\delta_j + p_j$ . Then, as argued above, when  $t_j$  is good,  $\|\bar{x}_{\sigma_{j+1}}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_{j+1}, \mathcal{R}_{j+1}]$  is satisfied. By the union bound, the probability that one of the steps fails (resetting the cycle before it completes) or all succeed but

## PDF page 34


one of the  $t_1, \ldots, t_{J-1}$  is bad, can be upper bounded by  $P + \Delta$ . In other words, with probability at least  $1 - P - \Delta$ , steps  $1, \ldots, j$  succeed and all outputs  $t_1, \ldots, t_{j-1}$  are good.

Now, we verify that the output of the algorithm solves the QLSP. With probability  $1 - P - \Delta$ , in any given cycle, step J is executed on input parameters satisfying  $\|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_{\sigma_J}\| = \|\boldsymbol{x}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_J, \mathcal{R}_J]$ , and it succeeds. In this scenario, Lemma 4 applies, and the ensemble  $\tilde{\rho}_{J,\text{good}}$  of outputs  $|\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_J\rangle\langle\bar{\boldsymbol{x}}_J|$  satisfies

$$\begin{aligned}\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}_{J, \rm good}\ket{\vec{x}} &\geq 1- \frac{2\eta_J^2}{(1-\eta_J)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_J^2+\mathcal{L}_J^2}{2\mathcal{L}_J^2}\right) \right)\end{aligned} \tag{139}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1591 -->

With probability at most  $P + \Delta$ , either the cycle does not reach the KP step (line 9), or it does reach the KP step but  $\|\bar{x}_{\sigma_J}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_J, \mathcal{R}_J]$  does not hold, and thus Lemma 4 does not apply. In this scenario, we may still say that the ensemble  $\tilde{\rho}_{J,\text{bad}}$  of outputs of step J (conditioned on an output existing) satisfies  $\langle \boldsymbol{x}|\tilde{\rho}_{J,\text{bad}}|\boldsymbol{x}\rangle \geq 0$ . Weighting these two ensembles by their (worst-case) probabilities, we conclude that the ensemble  $\tilde{\rho}_J$  of outputs of step J satisfies

$$\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}_J\ket{\vec{x}} \geq (1-P-\Delta)\bra{\vec{x}}\tilde{\rho}_{J, \rm good}\ket{\vec{x}} \geq  (1-P-\Delta)\left(1- \frac{2\eta_J^2}{(1-\eta_J)^2}\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\mathcal{R}_J^2+\mathcal{L}_J^2}{2\mathcal{L}_J^2}\right) \right) \right) \tag{140}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1597 -->

We now compute some bounds on the above using the particular parameter choices we have made, with the goal of showing that  $\langle \boldsymbol{x} | \tilde{\rho}_J | \boldsymbol{x} \rangle \geq 1 - \mu^2$ , with  $\mu$  given in Eq. (132). First, we note that by our definitions, for all j,  $\eta_j/(1+\eta_j) = \chi_j$ , and  $(1+\eta_j)/(1-\eta_j) = 1+2\chi_j$ . Furthermore, for  $j \geq 2$ , we have  $\mathcal{R}_j/\mathcal{L}_j \leq \hat{c}\hat{\beta}_{j-1}^2 = \hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2\hat{r}^{2J-2j}$ . For j=1, we have  $\mathcal{R}_1/\mathcal{L}_1 = \sigma_1^{-1} \leq \hat{c} \leq \hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2\hat{r}^{2J}$  (since  $\hat{\beta} > 1$  and  $\hat{r} > 1$ ). Thus, referencing Eq. (135) and substituting k=J-j-1, we have

$$P = \sum_{j=1}^{J-1} p_j \le \sum_{j=1}^{J-1} \left[ \frac{(1+\eta_j)^2}{(1-\eta_j)^2} \frac{4}{\beta_j^2 + 1} + \frac{2\eta_j^2 (2\ln(\mathcal{R}_j/\mathcal{L}_j) + 1 - 4\ln(\beta_j))}{(1-\eta_j)^2} \right] \tag{141}$$

$$\leq \sum_{j=1}^{J-1} \left[ 4(1+2\chi_j)^2 \beta_j^{-2} + 2\chi_j^2 \left( 2\ln\left(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}_{j-1}^2\right) + 1 - 4\ln(\beta_j) \right) \right] \tag{142}$$

$$= \sum_{i=1}^{J-1} \left[ 4(1+2\hat{\chi}\hat{q}^{-J+j})^2 \hat{\beta}^{-2} \hat{r}^{-2J+2j} + 2\hat{\chi}^2 \hat{q}^{-2J+2j} \left( 2\ln\left(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2 \hat{r}^{2J-2j}\right) + 1 - 4\ln\left(\hat{\beta}^2 \hat{r}^{J-j-1}\right) \right) \right] \tag{143}$$

$$= \sum_{k=0}^{J-2} \left[ 4(1+2\hat{\chi}\hat{q}^{-k-1})^2 \hat{\beta}^{-2}\hat{r}^{-2k} + 2\hat{\chi}^2 \hat{q}^{-2k-2} \ln(e\hat{c}^2\hat{r}^4) \right] \tag{144}$$

$$\leq \left[ \frac{4}{\hat{\beta}^2} \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \hat{r}^{-2k} \right] + \left[ \frac{16\hat{\chi}}{\hat{q}\hat{\beta}^2} \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \hat{r}^{-2k} \hat{q}^{-k} \right] + \left[ \frac{16\hat{\chi}^2}{\hat{q}^2 \hat{\beta}^2} \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \hat{r}^{-2k} \hat{q}^{-2k} \right] + \left[ \frac{2\hat{\chi}^2 \ln\left(e\hat{c}^2\hat{r}^4\right)}{\hat{q}^2} \sum_{k=0}^{\infty} \hat{q}^{-2k} \right] \tag{145}$$

$$= \frac{4}{\hat{\beta}^2 (1 - \hat{r}^{-2})} + \frac{16\hat{\chi}}{\hat{q}\hat{\beta}^2 (1 - \hat{r}^{-2}\hat{q}^{-1})} + \frac{16\hat{\chi}^2}{\hat{q}^2 \hat{\beta}^2 (1 - \hat{r}^{-2}\hat{q}^{-2})} + \frac{2\hat{\chi}^2 \ln(e\hat{c}^2\hat{r}^4)}{\hat{q}^2 (1 - \hat{q}^{-2})} \tag{146}$$

$$=\bar{P}\tag{147}$$

where  $\bar{P}$  was defined in Eq. (132). Similarly, working from Eq. (136), we bound  $\Delta$ , here utilizing the relation  $1/x \ge -1/\ln(1-x)$ , the substitution k = J - j + 1, and the definition of  $m_j$  in Eq. (132),

$$\begin{aligned}\Delta &= \sum_{j=1}^J \delta_j \leq \sum_{j=   }^J\left(1-\frac{(1-\eta_j)^2}{(1+\eta_j)^2(\ln(\mathcal{R}_j/\mathcal{L}_j)+1)}\right)^{m_j} \\
        &\leq \sum_{j=1}^J\left(1-\frac{(1-\eta_j)^2}{(1+\eta_j)^2\left(\ln(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2 \hat{r}^{2J-2j})+1\right)}\right)^{(J-j+1)\ln(

        &= \sum_{j=1}^J \left(\frac{\hat{\Delta}}{1+\hat{\Delta}}\right)^{J-j+1} \leq \sum_{k=1}^\infty \frac{\hat{\Delta}^k}{(1+\hat{\Delta})^k} = \hat{\Delta}\end{aligned}$$

$$=1-\mu^2$$

where  $\mu$  was defined in Eq. (132). The subsequent KP step can then be analyzed with Lemma 2. We observe that the output  $|x_J\rangle$  has no overlap with the kernel of A (see Theorem 1). Thus, Lemma 2 asserts that the output of the KP step satisfies

$$\frac{1}{2}\nrm{\ketbra{\vec{x}}-\tilde{\rho}}_1 \leq \frac{\mu\eta_{\rm KP}}{\sqrt{1-\mu^2+\mu^2\eta_{\rm KP}^2}} = \varepsilon\,, \tag{155}$$
