where the last equality is ensured by the choice of  $\eta_{KP}$  in Eq. (132). Thus we have verified that the ensemble output by the KP step, when it succeeds, solves the QLSP.

Next, we give an upper bound on the overall expected query complexity. Since each cycle is independent of the previous cycle, this query complexity is given by the expected number of cycles times the expected number of queries per cycle. The expected number of cycles is the inverse of the probability that all steps  $1, \ldots, J$  succeed, and then the KP step also succeeds. This quantity is at least the probability that all steps  $1, \ldots, J$  succeed and that all estimates  $t_1, \ldots, t_{J-1}$  are good, times the probability the KP step succeeds conditioned on the output  $\tilde{\rho}_{J,\text{good}}$ . This can then be lower bounded by  $(1 - P - \Delta)\langle \boldsymbol{x} | \tilde{\rho}_{J,\text{good}} | \boldsymbol{x} \rangle$ , which was itself lower bounded by  $1 - \mu^2$  above. Thus, we conclude that the expected number of cycles is at most  $(1 - \mu^2)^{-1}$ .

Let  $Q_j$  be the expected query complexity incurred by step j within any given cycle, for j = 1, ..., J, and let  $Q_{KP}$  be the expected query complexity incurred by the KP step (line 9) in any given cycle. We may then make the upper bound on the total expected query complexity


$$Q \leq \frac{\mathcal{Q}_{\rm KP} + \sum_{j=1}^J \mathcal{Q}_{j}}{1-\mu^2}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1650 -->

We now provide expressions upper bounding  $Q_j$  and  $Q_{KP}$ . We know that when step j executes, the probability that  $\|\bar{x}_{\sigma_j}\| \in [\mathcal{L}_j, \mathcal{R}_j]$  is at least  $1 - P - \Delta$ . When this is the case, each call to Algorithm 2 within step j succeeds with probability lower bounded by Eq. (82), and the expected number of calls to Algorithm 2 is at most the inverse of that quantity. With probability at most  $P + \Delta$ , we have no such guarantee, but we may still say that the expected number of calls to Algorithm 2 is  $m_j$ . Each time Algorithm 2 is called, it has a fixed query complexity equal to

## PDF page 36


 $2\lceil \sigma_i^{-1} \ln(2/\eta_i)/2 \rceil$ . Thus, in total we have


$$\mathcal{Q}_j \leq \left((1-P-\Delta)\frac{(1+\eta_j)^2\left(\ln(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}_{j-1}^2)+1\right)}{(1-\eta_j)^2} + (P+\Delta) m_j\right) 2\lceil \sigma_j^{-1} \ln(2/\eta_j)/2\rceil \tag{157}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1654 -->

On the other hand, the KP step executes at most once per cycle, and it has the same query complexity every time it executes, given by  $2\lceil \kappa \ln(2/\eta_{\rm KP})/2 \rceil$ . Thus we have


$$\mathcal{Q}_{\rm KP} \leq 2\lceil \kappa \ln(2/\eta_{\rm KP})/2\rceil \leq \kappa \ln(\frac{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2}}{\varepsilon}) - \kappa \ln(\frac{\sqrt{1-\mu^2}}{2\mu})+2$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1658 -->

where we have used the bound  $\lceil x \rceil \leq x + 1$  and substituted the definition of  $\eta_{\text{KP}}$  from Eq. (132).

We now update the bound on  $Q_j$  for our particular parameter choices. We note the relations  $(1+\eta_j)/(1-\eta_j) \le 1+2\hat{\chi}$  and  $1/\eta_j=(1+\chi_j)/\chi_j \le (1+\hat{\chi})/\chi_j=(1+\hat{\chi})\hat{q}^{J-j}/\hat{\chi}$ . We work from Eq. (157), using the definition of  $m_j$  in Eq. (132), to write

$$Q_{j} \leq \left(\frac{\ln\left(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^{2}\hat{r}^{2J-2j}\right)+1}{(1-\eta_{j})^{2}(1+\eta_{j})^{-2}}+(P+\Delta)\left(m_{j}-\frac{\ln\left(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^{2}\hat{r}^{2J-2j}\right)+1}{(1-\eta_{j})^{2}(1+\eta_{j})^{-2}}\right)\right)2\lceil\sigma_{j}^{-1}\ln(2/\eta_{j})/2\rceil \tag{159}$$

$$\leq \frac{\ln\left(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2\hat{r}^{2J-2j}\right)+1}{(1-\eta_j)^2(1+\eta_j)^{-2}}\left(1+(\bar{P}+\hat{\Delta})\left(\ln\left(\hat{\Delta}^{-1}+1\right)(J-j+1)-1\right)\right)\left(\sigma_j^{-1}\ln(2/\eta_j)+2\right) \tag{160}$$


$$= (1 + 2\hat{\chi})^2 \left( \ln \left( e\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2 \right) + (2J - 2j) \ln(\hat{r}) \right) \left( 1 + (\bar{P} + \hat{\Delta}) \left( \ln \left( \hat{\Delta}^{-1} + 1 \right) (J - j + 1) - 1 \right) \right)$$

$$\times \left( \kappa \hat{c}^{-J+j} \ln \left( 2\hat{\chi}^{-1} (1 + \hat{\chi}) \right) + \kappa \hat{c}^{-J+j} \ln(\hat{q}) (J - j) + 2 \right) \tag{161}$$

Working from this last expression, we take the sum over j and group terms by their j dependence, as follows.

$$\begin{aligned}\sum_{j=1}^J \mathcal{Q}_j 
    &\leq{} \kappa Z_0 \left[\sum_{j=1}^J \hat{c}^{-J+j}\right] + \kappa Z_1 \left[\sum_{j=1}^J (J-j)\hat{c}^{-J+j}\right] + \kappa Z_2 \left[\sum_{j=1}^J (J-j)^2\hat{c}^{-J+j}\right] \\
    & \qquad + \kappa Z_3\left[\sum_{j=1}^J (J-j)^3\hat{c}^{-J+j}\right] + JZ_4 + J^2 Z_5  \left[\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{J-j}{J^2} \right]+ J^3 Z_6 \left[\sum_{j=1}^J\frac{(J-j)^2}{J^3} \right]\end{aligned} \tag{162}$$

## F Lower bounds on the complexity of norm estimation

Our method has demonstrated that estimating the norm  $\|x\|$  to within a constant factor is a key step toward achieving an optimal QLSS with  $O(\kappa)$  complexity. One might hope that estimating the norm could be easier than producing the state  $|x\rangle$ . However, here we show an  $\Omega(\kappa)$  lower bound on estimating the norm to within a constant factor less than 5/4. Our proof extends the method in Ref. [13], which showed an  $\Omega(\kappa)$  lower bound on the query complexity of the QLSP even for positive semi-definite matrices.

The key idea of that method was to reduce the PromiseMajority problem to the QLSP, such that solving the QLSP yields a solution to PromiseMajority. Known lower bounds on PromiseMajority then imply a lower bound the QLSP. Here we do the same, modifying the construction of Ref. [13] so that we reduce to the problem of estimating the norm, rather than the QLSP. Technically, as stated, our bound leaves open the possibility of achieving a multiplicative-factor approximation worse than 5/4 in  $o(\kappa)$  complexity.

**Theorem 5.** Let  $\mathcal{A}$  be a quantum algorithm for esitmating the norm in the following sense. On any input  $\kappa \in [3, \infty)$ ,  $\varepsilon \in (0, 1/4]$ , and given (controlled) access to a (1, a)-block-encoding  $U_A$  for the  $N \times N$  matrix A with singular values contained in  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ , and state-preparation unitary  $U_{\mathbf{b}}$  for the N-dimensional vector  $\mathbf{b}$ , the algorithm  $\mathcal{A}$  outputs a value t, where, with probability at least 2/3,  $t \in [(1+\varepsilon)^{-1} ||\mathbf{x}||, (1+\varepsilon) ||\mathbf{x}||]$  where  $\mathbf{x}$  is the solution of minimum norm to the equation  $A\mathbf{x} = \mathbf{b}$ . Then  $\mathcal{A}$  must make at least

$$\Omega( \min(\kappa \varepsilon^{-1}, N))$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1739 -->

queries to  $U_A$  and to  $U_b$ .

Proof. First we define the PromiseMajority(M, N') problem following Ref. [13]: Given a vector  $\mathbf{y} \in \{0, 1\}^{N'}$  and a value of  $M \in \{1, \dots, N'\}$  (where M + N' is even), and the promise that either (i)  $y_i = 0$  for (N' + M)/2 of the entries, or (ii)  $y_i = 1$  for (N' + M)/2 of the entries, determine whether (i) or (ii) is the case. Suppose a quantum algorithm has query access to the entries of  $\mathbf{y}$  via a unitary  $\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{y}}$  acting as  $\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{y}}|i\rangle|z\rangle = |i\rangle|z \oplus y_i\rangle$ . Then, the number of queries to  $\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{y}}$  that the quantum algorithm must make to solve the PromiseMajority(M, N') problem with at least 2/3 probability of correctness is  $\Omega(N'/M)$  (see [13, Lemma 19] and [40, Corollary 1.2]).

Now, we will define a family of linear systems, parameterized by  $\kappa$ ,  $\varepsilon$ , and N. The linear systems will be size  $N \times N$  (without loss of generality, we let N be a power of 2) and have condition number  $\kappa$ . We will show that learning the norm of the solution to these linear systems to within factor  $1 + \varepsilon$  yields a solution to the PromiseMajority(M, N') problem, where the relationship between parameters (M, N') and  $(\kappa, \varepsilon, N)$  is

$$N' = N/2 \tag{179}$$

$$M = 2\max(1, 4N\varepsilon/\kappa). \tag{180}$$

Furthermore, we will show that the linear systems can be constructed such that the block-encoding  $U_A$  can be carried out with two queries to the oracle  $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{y}}$ , while  $U_{\boldsymbol{b}}$  requires zero queries; or it can be constructed such that  $U_A$  requires zero queries, and  $U_{\boldsymbol{b}}$  can be accomplished in one query. Thus, the lower bound of  $\Omega(N'/M)$  queries to  $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  will imply a query lower bound on the norm estimation problem of  $\Omega(\min(\kappa/\varepsilon, N))$ .

To define the family, let  $\mathbf{1} = (1; 1; \dots; 1)/\sqrt{N'}$  denote the unit vector of length N' with equal entries. Let D be the  $N' \times N'$  diagonal unitary matrix for which the *i*th diagonal entry of D is equal to  $(-1)^{y_i}$ , and define unit

## PDF page 39


vector

$$\vec{d} = D\vec{1} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{N'
        }}\begin{bmatrix}
            (-1)^{y_0} \\
            \vdots \\
            (-1)^{y_{N'-1}}
        \end{bmatrix}\,. \tag{181}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1755 -->

Finally, let

$$C = I_{N'} -(1-\kappa^{-1})\vec{1}\vec{1}^\dagger$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1764 -->

where  $I_{N'}$  is the  $N' \times N'$  identity matrix. We can see that all singular values of C lie in the interval  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ . We can also see that C is invertible and that its inverse is given by


$$C^{-1} = I_{N'} + (\kappa-1)\vec{1}\vec{1}^\dagger \,.$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1768 -->

Then, define the Hermitian  $N \times N$  matrix A and vector **b** by

$$A = 
       \frac{1}{2}\begin{bmatrix}
            C + I_{N'} & (C-I_{N'})D \\
            D(C-I_{N'}) & C+I_{N'}
        \end{bmatrix} \qquad \qquad  
        \vec{b} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{1 + \varepsilon^2N^2/M^2}}\begin{bmatrix}
            \vec{1} \\
            \varepsilon\frac{N}{M} \vec{1}
        \end{bmatrix}\,. \tag{184}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1773 -->

We note the factorizations

        \end{bmatrix}}_{\text{unitary}}
        %

        %

<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1785 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
       %
       %
       \vec{b} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{1 + \varepsilon^2N^2/M^2}}&\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
           I_{N'} & 0 \\
           0 & D

       %

<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1785 -->

Since all factors of A are unitary except for the third factor, and C has singular values in  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ , we conclude that A has singular values in  $[\kappa^{-1}, 1]$ . This also implies that A is invertible and Ax = b has a unique solution. Furthermore, from the factorization we see that this unique solution is

$$\begin{aligned}\vec{x} &= \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}{\sqrt{1 + \varepsilon^2N^2/M^2}}}\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
           I_{N'} & 0 \\

$$\begin{aligned}

Now we observe that the value of  $1^{\dagger}d$  differs in case (i) and case (ii) of the PromiseMajority problem.

$$\vec{1}^\dagger\vec{d} = \begin{cases}

        -M/N' = -2M/N & \text{in case (ii)}
    \end{cases} \tag{191}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1878 -->

Let  $\|x\|_{(i)}$  and  $\|x\|_{(ii)}$  denote the norm values in the two caes. We have

$$\frac{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{(i)}^{2}}{\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{(ii)}^{2}} = \frac{1+2\left(\varepsilon\frac{N}{M}\right)^{2} + \left(\kappa + 2\varepsilon(\kappa - 1)\right)^{2} - 4\varepsilon + 4\varepsilon\left(\kappa + 2\varepsilon(\kappa - 1)\right)}{1+2\left(\varepsilon\frac{N}{M}\right)^{2} + \left(\kappa - 2\varepsilon(\kappa - 1)\right)^{2} + 4\varepsilon - 4\varepsilon\left(\kappa - 2\varepsilon(\kappa - 1)\right)} \tag{192}$$

$$= \frac{\left(\kappa^2 + 1 + 2\left(\varepsilon\frac{N}{M}\right)^2 + 4\varepsilon^2(\kappa^2 - 1)\right) + 4\varepsilon(\kappa^2 - 1)}{\left(\kappa^2 + 1 + 2\left(\varepsilon\frac{N}{M}\right)^2 + 4\varepsilon^2(\kappa^2 - 1)\right) - 4\varepsilon(\kappa^2 - 1)} \tag{193}$$

$$= \frac{1 + \frac{4\frac{\kappa^2 - 1}{\kappa^2 + 1}\varepsilon}{1 + 2(\varepsilon \frac{N}{M})^2 \frac{1}{\kappa^2 + 1} + 4\frac{\kappa^2 - 1}{\kappa^2 + 1}\varepsilon^2}}{1 - \frac{4\frac{\kappa^2 - 1}{\kappa^2 + 1}\varepsilon}{1 + 2(\varepsilon \frac{N}{M})^2 \frac{1}{\kappa^2 + 1} + 4\frac{\kappa^2 - 1}{\kappa^2 + 1}\varepsilon^2}} \tag{194}$$

Now we recall some of our parameter relations. We have chosen M such that  $M \ge 4\varepsilon N/\kappa$ . We have assumed  $\kappa \ge 3$ , so  $(\kappa^2 - 1)/(\kappa^2 + 1) \ge 4/5$ . Finally, we have assumed  $\varepsilon \le 1/4$ , so  $4\frac{\kappa^2 - 1}{\kappa^2 + 1}\varepsilon^2 \le 1/4$ . This allows us to assert

$$\begin{aligned}\frac{\nrm{\vec{x}}_{(i)}^2}{\nrm{\vec{x}}_{(ii)}^2} &\geq 
    \frac{1+\frac{\frac{16}{5}\varepsilon}{1+\frac{1}{8} + \frac{1}{4}}}{1+\frac{\frac{16}{5}\varepsilon}{1+\frac{1}{8} + \frac{1}{4}}} \end{aligned} \tag{195}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1908 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
    &= \frac{1+\frac{128}{55}\varepsilon}{1-\frac{128}{55}\varepsilon}
    \end{aligned} \tag{196}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1908 -->




Now, if the algorithm  $\mathcal{A}$  produces an estimate t satisfying  $t \in [(1+\varepsilon)^{-1} ||x||, (1+\varepsilon) ||x||]$ , then


$$t^2 \in [(1+2\varepsilon+\varepsilon^2)^{-1} \nrm{\vec{x}}^2, (1+2\varepsilon +\varepsilon)\nrm{\vec{x}}^2] \subseteq [(1+9\varepsilon/4)^{-1}\nrm{\vec{x}}^2,(1+9\varepsilon/4)\nrm{\vec{x}}^2 ] \tag{197}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1916 -->

where the last inclusion follows under the assumption  $\varepsilon \leq 1/4$ . Since 9/4 < 128/55, Eqs. (196) and (197) together imply that by taking the output t and determining whether  $t^2$  is closer to  $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{(i)}^2$  or  $\|\boldsymbol{x}\|_{(ii)}^2$ , we can determine whether we are in case (i) or case (ii) and solve the PromiseMajority problem.

It remains to show that we can construct a block-encoding  $U_A$  and state-preparation untiary  $U_b$  for A and b as defined. Constructing  $U_b$  is simple as the vector does not depend on  $\mathbf{y}$  and need not query  $\mathcal{P}_{\mathbf{y}}$ , and all entries of the superposition are known. To construct  $U_A$ , we refer to its factorization in Eq. (185). The block-encoding for A multiplies block-encodings for each of the factors:

- The unitary operation  $\begin{bmatrix} I_{N'} & 0 \\ 0 & D \end{bmatrix} = |0\rangle\langle 0|\otimes I_{N'} + |1\rangle\langle 1|\otimes \mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  is accomplished with a controlled- $P_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  query. Controlled- $P_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  can be built from  $P_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  as follows. Prior to beginning the algorithm, we make make T = O(1) queries  $P_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  on random inputs  $i_1, \ldots, i_T$ , learning  $y_{i_1}, \ldots, y_{i_T}$ . With high probability we find a value  $i^*$  for which  $y_{i^*} = 0$  (unless  $M \approx N$  and we are in case (ii), in which case we can already easily differentiate case (i) and case (ii) in O(1) classical queries). We prepare the state  $|i^*\rangle$  in an ancilla register. In order to apply controlled- $P_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  on the state  $|i^*\rangle|c\rangle|i\rangle|z\rangle$ , where the first qubit is the ancilla, the second qubit is the control, and the final two qubits are the target of the query, we perform a controlled swap between the registers holding  $|i^*\rangle$  and  $|i\rangle$ , controlled on the register holding  $|c\rangle$ . Then, we query the final two registers, and undo the controlled swap. Since  $y_{i^*} = 0$ , there is no action on the final register when the control is set to 1.
- The unitary operation  $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\begin{bmatrix}I_{N'} & I_{N'}\\I_{N'} & -I_{N'}\end{bmatrix}$  is equivalent to a Hadamard gate,  $H\otimes I_{N'}$ . No queries to  $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  are required.
- The matrix  $\begin{bmatrix} C & 0 \\ 0 & I_{N'} \end{bmatrix} = |0\rangle\langle 0| \otimes C + |1\rangle\langle 1| \otimes I_{N'}$  is accomplished by controlled- $U_C$  operation, where  $U_C$  is a blockencoding of C. We block-encode C as follows. Let  $\theta = \arccos(\kappa^{-1})$ . First, perform a parallel layer of Hadmard gates, which maps  $|\mathbf{1}\rangle$  to  $|\mathbf{e_0}\rangle$ . Then, introduce an ancilla qubit and apply  $|\mathbf{e_0}\rangle\langle \mathbf{e_0}| \otimes e^{i\theta Y} + (I_{N'} |0\rangle\langle 0|) \otimes I_2$ . Finally, apply another layer of Hadamards to send  $|0\rangle$  back to  $|\mathbf{1}\rangle$ . Since  $\langle 0|e^{i\theta Y}|0\rangle = \kappa^{-1}$ , we have the correct  $\langle \mathbf{1}|C|\mathbf{1}\rangle$  matrix element. For any vector orthogonal to  $|\mathbf{1}\rangle$ , the  $R_y$  gate is not triggered, so  $U_C$  acts as identity.

## PDF page 41


Overall, each  $U_A$  operation requires two  $\mathcal{P}_{\boldsymbol{y}}$  queries, and each  $U_{\boldsymbol{b}}$  operation requires no queries. This completes the proof that the number of queries to  $U_A$  must be at least  $\Omega(\min(\kappa \varepsilon^{-1}, N))$ .

The method above does not lower bound the number of times A must query the state-preparation unitary  $U_b$ . To make this lower bound, we slightly modify the construction to

        \end{bmatrix}}_{\text{unitary}}
        %

           C & 0 \\
           0 & I_{N'} 
       \end{bmatrix} 
       %
        \underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}
           I_{N'}/\sqrt{2} & I_{N'}/\sqrt{2} \\
           I_{N'}/\sqrt{2} & -I_{N'}/\sqrt{2}
        \end{bmatrix}}_{\text{unitary}}
        \end{aligned} \tag{198}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1940 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
       %
       %
       \vec{b} = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}\sqrt{1 + \varepsilon^2N^2/M^2}}
       &\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}

           0 & D
        \end{bmatrix}}_{\text{unitary}}

            \vec{1}\\

        \end{bmatrix}\end{aligned} \tag{199}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1940 -->

Here, the block-encoding  $U_A$  requires zero queries to  $\mathcal{P}_{y}$ , and the unitary  $U_b$  can be implemented with one query. Furthermore, the solution x is unitarily related to the solution stated above, and thus has the same norm in case (i) and case (ii). The same analysis then implies that the number of queries to the state preparation unitary cannot be smaller than  $\Omega(\kappa \varepsilon^{-1}, N)$ . П

#### G Optimal QLSS based on quantum Zeno effect

In this section, we sketch an analysis for an optimal QLSS based on the Zeno method. This is essentially the same as the near-optimal method from Ref. [8], applied to the adiabatic path Section 5.3, and incorporating fixed-point amplitude amplification and log log trick to achieve optimality.

We assume without loss of generality that  $\kappa$  is a power of 2. First, we note the identity that when  $\kappa^{-1} \leq \varsigma \leq 1$ and  $\kappa^{-1} \leq \sigma < \sigma' \leq 1$ , we have

$$\frac{f(\sigma)f(\sigma') + \varsigma^2 \sqrt{(1 - f(\sigma)^2)(1 - f(\sigma')^2)}}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2(1 - f(\sigma')^2)} \ge \frac{f(\sigma)f(\sigma') + \varsigma^2(1 - f(\sigma')^2)}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2(1 - f(\sigma')^2)} \tag{200}$$

$$= \frac{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2 (1 - f(\sigma')^2) - f(\sigma') (f(\sigma') - f(\sigma))}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2 (1 - f(\sigma')^2)}, \tag{201}$$

$$= 1 - (f(\sigma') - f(\sigma)) \frac{f(\sigma')}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2 (1 - f(\sigma')^2)} \tag{202}$$

$$\geq 1 - (f(\sigma') - f(\sigma)) \frac{f(\sigma')}{\sigma'^2} \tag{203}$$


$$\geq 1 - \frac{f(\sigma') - f(\sigma)}{\sigma'} \tag{204}$$

where the first line uses monotonicity of f (i.e.,  $f(\sigma) < f(\sigma')$ ), the second to last line uses the identity  $f(\sigma')^2 + \kappa^{-2}(1 - f(\sigma')^2) = \sigma'^2$  and the fact that  $\varsigma \ge \kappa^{-1}$ , and the final line uses  $\sigma' \ge f(\sigma')$ . The key fact is that the overlap between nearby states along the adiabatic path remains close to one (see Section 5.3 for definitions):

$$\begin{aligned}\lvert \braket{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}}}{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}} \rvert &= 
    %
    \frac{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}^\dagger \vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}} }{\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}}\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}}}} \geq 
    %
    \frac{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}^\dagger \vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}} }{\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}}}^2}
    %
    =  \frac{\sum_{j} |w_j|^2\frac{f(\sigma)f(\sigma') + \varsigma_j^2\sqrt{(1-f(\sigma)^2)(1-f(\sigma')^2)}}{(f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2) \varsigma_j^2)(f(\sigma')^2 + (1-f(\sigma')^2) \varsigma_j^2)}}{\sum_j \frac{|w_j|^2}{f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2)\varsigma_j^2}  } \\
    &\geq  \left(1-\frac{f(\sigma')-f(\sigma)}{\sigma'}\right)\frac{\sum_{j} \frac{|w_j|^2}{f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2) \varsigma_j^2}}{\sum_j \frac{|w_j|^2}{f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2)\varsigma_j^2}  } \\
    %
    &= 1-\frac{f(\sigma')-f(\sigma)}{\sigma'}\,.\end{aligned}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 1986 -->



$$(205)$$

$$\geq \left(1 - \frac{f(\sigma') - f(\sigma)}{\sigma'}\right) \frac{\sum_{j} \frac{|w_{j}|^{2}}{f(\sigma)^{2} + (1 - f(\sigma)^{2})\varsigma_{j}^{2}}}{\sum_{j} \frac{|w_{j}|^{2}}{f(\sigma)^{2} + (1 - f(\sigma)^{2})\varsigma_{i}^{2}}}$$

$$(206)$$

$$=1-\frac{f(\sigma')-f(\sigma)}{\sigma'}. (207)$$

## PDF page 42


where we have used the identity of Eq. (204) in the second line. Note that since  $\sigma' \geq f(\sigma')$ , the right-hand side is greater than  $f(\sigma)/f(\sigma')$ . When  $\sigma, \sigma' \geq 2\kappa^{-1}$ ,  $f(\sigma) \approx \sigma$  holds reasonably well, so we may roughly think of the overlap as being lower bounded by approximately  $\sigma/\sigma'$ , as mentioned in the main text.

We may now construct a Zeno-inspired algorithm using a similar sequence of  $\sigma$  values as was used for norm estimation in Section 5.3. There, we chose the geometric sequence:  $1, 2^{-1}, 2^{-2}, \dots, 2^{-\log_2(\kappa)} \equiv \kappa^{-1}$ . Here, we choose the same sequence with the inverse of f applied, appended with a final point where  $\sigma = \kappa^{-1}$ :

$$\begin{aligned}1\equiv f^{-1}(1), f^{-1}(2^{-1}), f^{-1}(2^{-2}), \ldots, f^{-1}(\kappa^{-1}), f^{-1}(0.5\kappa^{-1}), f^{-1}(0) \equiv \kappa^{-1}\end{aligned} \tag{208}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 2001 -->

This sequence has the property that the overlap between subsequent states in the sequence is always lower bounded by 1/2. We can see this by applying the identity in Eq. (207) and obtaining

$$\begin{aligned}\lvert \braket{\vec{\bar{x}_{f^{-1}(2^{-j})}}}{\vec{\bar{x}_{f^{-1}(2^{-j-1})}}} \rvert \geq  \frac{f(f^{-1}(2^{-j-1}))}{f(f^{-1}(2^{-j}))}= \frac{1}{2}\end{aligned} \tag{209}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 2005 -->

and also (for the last jump in the sequence)

$$\begin{aligned}\lvert \braket{\vec{\bar{x}_{f^{-1}(0.5\kappa^{-1})}}}{\vec{\bar{x}_{f^{-1}(0)}}} \rvert \geq 1-\frac{0.5\kappa^{-1}}{f^{-1}(0.5\kappa^{-1})} \geq \frac{1}{2}\,,\end{aligned} \tag{210}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 2009 -->

where the last inequality is true since the function  $f^{-1}$  maps all inputs to values greater than  $\kappa^{-1}$ .

The main idea of the Zeno algorithm is to jump from one state in the sequence to the next. Namely, given that we have prepared the state  $|\bar{x}_{\sigma'}\rangle$ , we approximately implement the jump  $|\bar{x}_{\sigma'}\rangle \mapsto |\bar{x}_{\sigma}\rangle$  by fixed-point amplitude amplification [24]; this requires the ability to reflect about both  $|\bar{x}_{\sigma'}\rangle$  and  $|\bar{x}_{\sigma'}\rangle$ , which is supplied by KR to error  $\delta/2$  at cost  $O(\sigma^{-1}\log(1/\delta))$ . Since the overlap is lower bounded by a constant, we may amplify the jump to success probability  $1 - \delta/2$  at cost  $O(\log(1/\delta))$  calls to KR. Noting that  $\sigma \geq f(\sigma)$ , we may conclude that each jump is performed to error  $\delta$  at cost  $O(f(\sigma)^{-1}\log^2(1/\delta))$ . As in the analysis in Section 5.3, we invoke the log log trick, choosing the value of  $\delta$  on the jump from  $f^{-1}(2^{-j})$  to  $f^{-1}(2^{-j-1})$  to be  $e^{\Omega(2+\log_2(\kappa)-j)}$ , and choosing the value of  $\delta$  on the final jump to be O(1). The total error over the  $\log_2(\kappa) + 2$  jumps is at most O(1). The total cost is given by the sum (cf. Eq. (42))

$$\begin{aligned}Q&=\underbrace{O(\kappa)}_{\text{final jump}} + \sum_{j=1}^{\log_2(\kappa)+1} O(2^j(2+\log_2(\kappa)-j)^2) \end{aligned} \tag{211}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 2016 -->

$$\begin{aligned}
 &\leq  O(\kappa) \sum_{j'=0}^{\infty } 2^{-j'}(1+j')^2
 \leq O(\kappa)\,.\end{aligned} \tag{212}$$
<!-- authors' LaTeX: main.tex line 2016 -->



This shows that a Zeno-based QLSS equipped with fixed-point amplitude amplification and the log log trick can achieve optimal complexity.


### Display 48: `equation` at `main.tex` line 949

```latex
T_\ell(z) = \begin{cases}
        \cos(\ell \arccos(z)) & \text{if } |z|\leq 1 \\
        \cosh(\ell \arccosh(z)) & \text{if } z >1 \\
        (-1)^\ell \cosh(\ell \arccosh(z)) & \text{if } z < -1
    \end{cases}\,.
```

### Display 49: `align` at `main.tex` line 972


### Display 105: `equation` at `main.tex` line 1513, label `eq:optimal_QLSS_param_relations`

```latex
\label{eq:optimal_QLSS_param_relations}
\begin{split}
    J &= \lceil \ln(\kappa)/ \ln(\hat{c}    )\rceil \\
    \sigma_j &= \kappa^{-1}\hat{c}^{J-j} \in [\kappa^{-1},1] \\
    \beta_j &= \hat{\beta}\hat{r}^{J-j-1} \\
    \chi_j &= \hat{\chi} \hat{q}^{-J+j} \\
    \eta_j &= \chi_j / (1+\chi_j) \\ 
    m_j &= (J-j+1)\ln(
         \hat{\Delta}^{-1}+1)\frac{(1+\eta_j)^2(\ln(\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2 \hat{r}^{2J-2j})+1)}{(1-\eta_j)^2}  \\
    \bar{P} &= \frac{4}{\hat{\beta}^2(1-\hat{r}^{-2})}
        +\frac{16\hat{\chi}}{\hat{q}\hat{\beta}^2(1-\hat{r}^{-2}\hat{q}^{-1})}
        +\frac{16\hat{\chi}^2}{\hat{q}^2\hat{\beta}^2(1-\hat{r}^{-2}\hat{q}^{-2})}
        +  \frac{2\hat{\chi}^2\ln(e\hat{c}^2\hat{r}^4)}{\hat{q}^2-1} \\
    \mu &= \sqrt{1-\left(1-\hat{\Delta} - \bar{P}\right)\left(1 - 2\hat{\chi}^2\left(\frac{3}{2} + \ln\left(\frac{\hat{c}\hat{\beta}^2+1}{2}\right) \right)\right)}\\
    \eta_{\mathrm KP} &= \frac{\varepsilon}{\mu}\frac{\sqrt{1-\mu^2}}{\sqrt{1-\varepsilon^2}} 
\end{split}
```

### Display 140: `align` at `main.tex` line 1977, label `eq:Zeno_identity`

```latex
\frac{f(\sigma)f(\sigma') + \varsigma^2 \sqrt{(1-f(\sigma)^2)(1-f(\sigma')^2)}}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2(1-f(\sigma')^2)} &\geq \frac{f(\sigma)f(\sigma') + \varsigma^2 (1-f(\sigma')^2) }{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2(1-f(\sigma')^2)}  \\
    %
    &= \frac{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2 (1-f(\sigma')^2)  - f(\sigma')(f(\sigma')-f(\sigma))}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2(1-f(\sigma')^2)}\,, \\
    &= 1- (f(\sigma') - f(\sigma))\frac{f(\sigma')}{f(\sigma')^2 + \varsigma^2(1-f(\sigma')^2)} \\
    &\geq 1- (f(\sigma') - f(\sigma))\frac{f(\sigma')}{\sigma'^2} \\
    &\geq 1- \frac{f(\sigma') - f(\sigma)}{\sigma'} \label{eq:Zeno_identity}
```

### Display 141: `align` at `main.tex` line 1986, label `eq:overlap_zeno`

```latex
\lvert \braket{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}}}{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}} \rvert &= 
    %
    \frac{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}^\dagger \vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}} }{\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}}\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}}}} \geq 
    %
    \frac{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma'}}^\dagger \vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}} }{\nrm{\vec{\bar{x}_{\sigma}}}^2}
    %
    =  \frac{\sum_{j} |w_j|^2\frac{f(\sigma)f(\sigma') + \varsigma_j^2\sqrt{(1-f(\sigma)^2)(1-f(\sigma')^2)}}{(f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2) \varsigma_j^2)(f(\sigma')^2 + (1-f(\sigma')^2) \varsigma_j^2)}}{\sum_j \frac{|w_j|^2}{f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2)\varsigma_j^2}  } \\
    &\geq  \left(1-\frac{f(\sigma')-f(\sigma)}{\sigma'}\right)\frac{\sum_{j} \frac{|w_j|^2}{f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2) \varsigma_j^2}}{\sum_j \frac{|w_j|^2}{f(\sigma)^2 + (1-f(\sigma)^2)\varsigma_j^2}  } \\
    %
    &= 1-\frac{f(\sigma')-f(\sigma)}{\sigma'}\,. \label{eq:overlap_zeno}
```
